# PHOENIX / NBT — UNIFIED MASTER GAME FRANCHISE PROJECT
## Universal Game-Logic World, Product Family, Hardware Platform & Development Bible

**Project date:** 19 August 2026  
**Status:** Master synthesis / foundation specification  
**Primary IP:** Phoenix / NBT: The Sovereign Reality  
**Franchise principle:** One coherent universe + one universal state-transition architecture + many specialized games and products.

# 0. INTEGRITY NOTICE

This project consolidates the two attached source files:

1. `gaming logics.txt`
2. `merged wiyhout logic file.txt`

The source files are preserved verbatim in the appendices of this project file so that the synthesis does not silently delete source material. The master design before the appendices is an **organized architectural synthesis**, not a claim that every historical game ever created has been literally enumerated.

The source material itself correctly identifies the technically defensible interpretation of “100% coverage”: represent every identifiable fundamental game-logic family, algorithmic family, information model, randomness model, pattern family, action type, resource relationship, strategic interaction and victory structure needed to reconstruct or generate games across the identified domains. A literal guarantee of every game ever made is not mathematically provable. fileciteturn1file2L210-L276

# 1. THE FRANCHISE IN ONE SENTENCE

**Phoenix is a game-world and universal game platform in which spatial geometry, formal action syntax, probability, hidden information, physical kinetics, economy, social interaction, progression, exploration and narrative are all expressed through a common auditable state-transition system, allowing one universe to contain dozens to hundreds of distinct games without reducing them to a superficial mash-up.**

The key architectural rule is:

> Do not build “Chess + Sudoku + UNO + Tetris + Pokémon.” Build a universal state-transition system capable of expressing those families as configurations. fileciteturn1file6L547-L607

---

# 2. FRANCHISE HIERARCHY

## 2.1 Layer 0 — NBT Civilizational Canon

The wider NBT/Bharat framework is the civilization-scale setting: governance, justice, economy, education, health/longevity, infrastructure, environment, science, ocean/space, creativity, diplomacy, disaster resilience, AI governance, rights/ethics, culture, continuity and human purpose.

Named civilization systems retained in the franchise canon include:

- UCLT — Universal Case Lifecycle Template
- DNN — Decentralized National Nodes
- AuI / thermodynamic currency concepts
- Thermodynamic Economics
- Aerotropolis
- NBT Gauntlet / Garuda Purana trial framework
- Lemniscate / equilibrium concepts
- Reference Compression
- Chitragupta Engine
- Saptabhagini Engine
- Sovereign Reality Architecture

The game uses these as fictionalized world systems and gameplay abstractions, not as claims that the described real-world technologies or medical/social outcomes presently exist.

## 2.2 Layer 1 — Phoenix Universe

The narrative world contains:

- The Aerotropolis / Real-World Heaven
- The NBT Gauntlet / Real-World Hell
- NB Islands
- Dead Zones
- Vertical Cell Block Towers
- Core Hubs and concentric cities
- Frontier Walls
- The Void
- Metaverse spectator civilization
- God Channel
- Chitragupta Engine
- Garuda Purana sectors
- Phoenix Protocol
- Metaphysical Somersault / Moksha Protocol

The source describes Aerotropolis ring cities as 120-story structures slanted at 15 degrees, and NBT Gauntlet Dead Zones as 50-square-mile environments containing vertical towers and the artificial NB Islands project. fileciteturn2file0L16-L34

## 2.3 Layer 2 — Three Master Engines

### A. Vitruvian Engine
Spatial/structural engine.

Responsibilities:
- coordinates
- grids
- polyomino placement
- collision
- enclosure
- load-bearing
- line of sight
- territory
- dynamic board geometry
- Dynamic Quadtrees
- physical board topology

The source defines it as a non-predictive processor treating movement and terrain as geometry and coordinates. fileciteturn2file0L36-L50

### B. Pāṇinian Engine
Action-language/compiler engine.

Canonical syntax:

**[Modifier] + [Action] + [Target]**

Responsibilities:
- legal action syntax
- modifiers
- targets
- card programming
- simultaneous selection
- compilation failure
- LIFO interrupts
- dynamic rule modification
- semantic/pattern processing

The flagship prototype uses a 9-card neural buffer and 30-card role decks. fileciteturn2file0L796-L850

### C. Saptabhagini Engine
Meta-state, ledger and progression engine.

Responsibilities:
- Bleed-Rate Wallet
- Comfort Tax
- Karmic Debt
- Neck-Band/Tension state
- progression
- automated event/punishment rules
- spectator interactions
- cross-product ledger
- rewards
- account state
- audit

The flagship baseline specifies a passive 15-credit per-turn drain, tension from 1–10, a 10-second simultaneous action window, and a Cut action that resets tension. fileciteturn2file0L715-L751

# 3. UNIVERSAL GAME SCHEMA

Every Phoenix game is represented as:

**GAME = STATE + PLAYERS + RULES + ACTIONS + INFORMATION + RESOURCES + RANDOMNESS + TIME + OBJECTIVES + OUTCOME + PATTERNS + ALGORITHMS + WORLD**

Canonical state transition:

**S(t+1) = F(S(t), A(t), E(t), R(t))**

where:
- S(t) = current state
- A(t) = player/AI action
- E(t) = environmental/event state
- R(t) = random outcome
- F = complete rules transition function

Probability is represented separately:

**P(R(t) | S(t), A(t))**

This separation is essential: one function defines what can happen; the probability model defines how likely each outcome is. fileciteturn1file6L559-L607

---

# 4. FIVE-LEVEL LOGIC MODEL

Every mechanic is decomposed into:

| Level | Question | Phoenix example |
|---|---|---|
| L1 Primitive | What happened? | A card moved |
| L2 Rule | Was it legal? | Card syntax valid |
| L3 Pattern | What structure resulted? | Valid 3-card program |
| L4 Strategy | Why is it valuable? | Preserves future options |
| L5 Outcome | What changed? | Rival loses a turn |

This prevents superficial mechanic copying and makes the game generator capable of reasoning about the strategic meaning of mechanics. fileciteturn2file1L1311-L1328

# 7. MATHEMATICAL FOUNDATION

## 7.1 Probability

P(A) = favorable outcomes / total outcomes

P(not A) = 1 - P(A)

Independent:
P(A ∩ B) = P(A)P(B)

Conditional:
P(A | B) = P(A ∩ B) / P(B)

Bayesian:
P(A | B) = P(B | A)P(A) / P(B)

## 7.2 Permutation

n! possible orderings of n unique objects.

For a 52-card deck:

**52! ≈ 8.0658 × 10^67**

## 7.3 Combination

C(n,r) = n! / (r!(n-r)!)

A standard 52-card deck has:

**C(52,5) = 2,598,960**

possible 5-card hands.

## 7.4 Expected value

E[X] = Σ xᵢP(xᵢ)

The engine uses EV to distinguish:
- safe action
- risky action
- extreme action
- deterministic optimum
- probabilistic optimum

## 7.5 Risk

Risk = P(Failure) × Loss

Reward/risk can be represented as a tunable utility ratio.

## 7.6 Distributions

The generator must support:
- uniform
- weighted
- Bernoulli
- binomial
- multinomial
- geometric
- negative binomial
- hypergeometric
- Poisson
- normal
- exponential
- custom distributions

## 7.7 Hypergeometric

Required for card draws and finite populations without replacement.

## 7.8 Information entropy

InformationGain = H(before) − H(after)

This powers Mastermind-style optimal questioning and information-first strategies. fileciteturn2file1L1806-L1828

# 9. RANDOMNESS AND REPRODUCIBILITY

Phoenix does **not** use a single generic random function.

Every stochastic event records:
- seed
- distribution
- parameters
- state before
- action
- event
- probability
- state after

Same seed + same actions must produce the same state.

Generation pipeline:

**Seed → Board Generator → Piece Generator → Deck Generator → Enemy Generator → Objective Generator → Probability Generator → Difficulty Generator → Victory Generator → Playable Game**

fileciteturn2file1L922-L944

---

# 10. GAME GENERATOR

The generator works as:

**Theme → Player Model → Board Model → Mechanics → Patterns → Algorithms → Information Model → Time Model → Probability Model → Objectives → Economy → Progression → Difficulty → Simulation → Balance → Package**

It must reject incompatible combinations rather than blindly combine everything.

The source's illustrative design space already reaches:

100 mechanics × 20 board types × 15 information models × 10 victory models × 10 time models × 10 probability models = **30,000,000 structural combinations**, before parameters such as board size, players, pieces, cards, rules, objectives, AI, maps and progression. fileciteturn2file1L946-L1013

---

# 11. VALIDATION / BALANCE ENGINE

Every generated game must test:

- rule consistency
- reachability
- termination
- solvability
- balance
- probability normalization
- fairness
- exploit detection
- dominant strategy
- deadlock
- difficulty
- replayability
- AI solvability
- economy stability
- runtime

Monte Carlo testing becomes the default empirical balance layer. Example metrics include win rate, duration, draw rate, score distribution and strategy success rate. fileciteturn2file1L1015-L1054

---

# 12. “100% INTEGRITY” STANDARD

The franchise defines integrity as measurable properties:

### Coverage
C = represented identified mechanic classes / identified mechanic classes

### Rule integrity
R = valid tested rules / total rules

### Probability integrity
ΣP(outcome) = 1

### State integrity
Every state belongs to ValidStates.

### Reproducibility
(seed, actions) → deterministic state for a fixed version.

### Auditability
Every event records:
- timestamp
- player
- action
- previous state
- random seed
- random event
- probability
- rule triggered
- new state
- result

This is the source-defined technical interpretation of auditable “100% integrity.” fileciteturn2file1L1056-L1102

# 13. FLAGSHIP — PHOENIX: ARENA CORE

## 13.1 Core format

- 4 players
- asymmetric roles
- semi-cooperative
- engine building
- survival
- 60–120 minutes
- 4 Naraka rounds
- modular expanding ring board
- mixed perfect/partial/hidden information
- controlled stochasticity
- 7-bag/hypergeometric systems
- central swappable hub
- electromagnetic/kinetic card hardware
- projection
- physical audio/rumble

## 13.2 Four roles

### Strategist — White Knight
Pāṇinian manipulation, auditing, wallet redirection, sacrifice and rule control.

### Builder — Black Rook
Vitruvian geometry, polyomino drafting, interlocking construction, load-bearing and structural defense.

### Healer — White Bishop
Natural Way, flora, ancestral cycles, emotional triage and karmic restoration.

### Disruptor — Black Pawn
Scientific Way, optical stealth, hacking, spectator manipulation and sabotage.

Each role has a 30-card starter deck with 9 Modifier, 9 Action, 9 Target and 3 Cut cards. fileciteturn1file0L157-L210

---

# 14. FLAGSHIP PLAYER STATE

### Bleed-Rate Wallet
Baseline Comfort Tax: 15 credits per turn.

At zero:
- Reversal Protocol
- shield removal
- hunting-drone alert

### Neck-Band
1–10 tension meter.

At 10:
- next turn lost

### 1-Second / 1-Thought fiction
Implemented as a physical 10-second simultaneous-selection phase.

### The Cut
Blank/Cut card:
- intentionally does nothing
- resets tension
- prevents escalation

These are retained as the core “body/economy/time” triangle. fileciteturn1file0L212-L230

---

# 15. NATURAL VS SCIENTIFIC PROGRESSION

### Natural Way
- flora gathering
- campfire nodes
- poultices
- ancestral cycles
- autophagy/fasting as fictional game mechanics
- lower long-term debt
- slower progression

### Scientific Way
- nanite stims
- corporate drops
- instant healing
- temporary action amplification
- increased karmic debt/tension

The two paths are intentionally asymmetric and create a strategic economy rather than a simple good/bad binary. fileciteturn2file0L746-L751

# 16. FOUR NARAKA SECTORS

| Round | Sector | Rule | Primary logic |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Tamisram | Darkness | Hidden information + CSP + memory |
| 2 | Rauravam | Silence | Spatial packing + cooperation |
| 3 | Kumbhipakam | Thermal cascade | Risk/reward + spectator disruption |
| 4 | Asipattravana | 4D portal bleed | Chess search + territory + sacrifice |

The first round uses UCLT cards and limited communication; the second uses silent cooperative construction; the third activates God Channel spectator effects; the fourth becomes a shifting PvPvE chess-like board. fileciteturn2file0L753-L779

# 17. METAPHYSICAL SOMERSAULT / MOKSHA ENDGAME

The flagship deliberately does **not** end by simply eliminating three players.

Required chain:

1. Build the NB Islands bridge.
2. Reach the final danger zone.
3. One player voluntarily sacrifices individual point supremacy.
4. Their wallet reaches zero.
5. The Saptabhagini logic recognizes the collective solution.
6. Neck-Bands unlatch.
7. Ledger keys distribute to survivors.

Failure:
- cooperation fails, or
- collective karmic debt exceeds structural threshold,
- NB Islands collapse,
- global Reversal Protocol triggers.

fileciteturn2file0L781-L794

# 18. PĀṆINIAN CARD COMPILER

## 18.1 Hand

9-card Neural Buffer:
- 3 target/subject
- 3 action
- 3 modifier

## 18.2 Syntax

Primary:
**Modifier + Action + Target**

Fast:
**Action + Target**

Invalid syntax:
- compilation failure
- cards lost
- no resource
- tension +1

## 18.3 Interrupts

LIFO stack.

A later/high-priority modifier can resolve before an earlier action, unless blocked by a suitable firewall/nullification modifier.

The source explicitly defines syntax failure and LIFO resolution. fileciteturn2file0L827-L850

---

# 19. VITRUVIAN POLYOMINO SYSTEM

Use the seven tetromino forms:

I, O, T, L, J, S, Z

Physical baseline:
- opaque bag
- 7-bag logic
- four sets / 28-piece cycle in the flagship prototype
- five-piece face-up runway
- drafting requires a compiled Build/Extract action

The Pāṇinian sequence can determine:
- which piece may be drafted
- density
- rotation
- coordinate
- elevation
- ally relationship
- structural consequence

Examples retained from the source:
- Stealth + Build + NB Island Node
- Double + Build + Self
- Shared + Build + Rival
- Lethal + Extract + NB Island Node

fileciteturn2file0L852-L910

# 20. THE BOARD AS A LIVING WORLD

The board is not a fixed board.

It begins with:
**Core Hub**

Then expands through:
- inner ring
- industrial ring
- frontier wall
- outer/void layer
- new modules triggered by player count
- new modules triggered by world events

The earlier board architecture explicitly proposed 2-player inner rings and larger attached loops for 4–6 players, with walls controlling movement, line of sight and military control. fileciteturn2file0L307-L339

### Layer 1 — Spatial Geometry
Tetris + Sudoku + Magic Square.

Mancala/Pallanguzhi + Solitaire + Bingo.

### Layer 3 — Tactical Movement
Chess + Kabaddi.

Poker + Rummy + UNO.

Pokémon GO-style AR/location/progression.

The original integrated loop is retained conceptually: a constraint can govern a spatial placement, which enables a timed raid, which changes resources, which triggers pattern completion, which unlocks cards, which reveals a hidden threat, which can trigger a physical/AR investigation. fileciteturn2file0L383-L470

| 19 | **Phoenix: Minesweeper Frontier** | 1–4 player | Probabilistic exploration | Local constraint propagation + Bayesian heatmaps | Map the frontier while minimizing catastrophic risk |

# 34. AI ARCHITECTURE

AI chooses its algorithm based on game structure.

| Problem | Preferred method |
|---|---|
| Chess-like deterministic search | Minimax + alpha-beta |
| Large branching stochastic game | MCTS |
| Sudoku/CSP | CSP + propagation + backtracking |
| Hidden information | Bayesian inference |
| Navigation | A*/Dijkstra |
| Local path search | BFS/DFS |
| Probability-heavy game | Monte Carlo |
| Optimization | DP / branch-and-bound |
| Adaptive opponents | RL / policy-value |
| Procedural content | constraints + procedural generation |
| Opponent modeling | Bayesian/statistical prediction |
| Mixed puzzle/tactical game | MCTS + CSP hybrid |

---

# 35. DYNAMIC ENCLOSURE / QUADTREE ARCHITECTURE

The board cannot rely on an unbounded flood fill.

The validated design is:

1. Build an ephemeral evaluation graph.
2. Calculate bounding box.
3. Add a one-coordinate leak threshold.
4. Run BFS on a temporary visited set.
5. Abort if the leak threshold is reached.
6. Treat existing geometry as walls.
7. Return an immutable `EnclosureResolvedDTO`.
8. Apply via a pure reducer to produce a new immutable game state.

This keeps enclosure evaluation bounded, deterministic and isolated from mutable domain state. fileciteturn2file0L594-L650

# 38. DESIGN COMPATIBILITY GRAPH

Each mechanic is a node.

Edges have:
- compatibility score
- thematic compatibility
- computational cost
- cognitive load
- physical feasibility
- player-count suitability
- information compatibility
- probability compatibility
- economy compatibility
- balance risk
- narrative compatibility

The generator can therefore say:

**Allowed**
- Tetris + territory
- Chess + economy
- Poker + hidden information
- Sudoku + combat constraints

**Conditional**
- real-time + deep CSP
- high physical dexterity + accessibility
- large hidden-state + deterministic fairness

**Rejected**
- combinations that create unsolvable, unfair, unsafe or computationally pathological games.

# 48. WHAT THE FRANCHISE ACTUALLY IS

Phoenix has four simultaneous identities:

### 1. A universe
A coherent NBT civilization / Gauntlet / Metaverse narrative.

### 2. A flagship game
Phoenix: Arena Core.

### 3. A platform
Omni-Board + Smart Cards + Cryptex + AR + digital twin.

### 4. A game-generation system
A universal ontology capable of expressing many game families through shared mathematical and computational primitives.

That is the central strategic architecture.

---

# 49. NON-NEGOTIABLE DESIGN RULE

**Do not force every mechanic into every game.**

Every individual Phoenix game should select a coherent subset from the universal engine.

The franchise achieves breadth through the platform, while each game achieves depth through a focused mechanic profile.

This preserves:
- identity
- learnability
- balance
- replayability
- performance
- thematic coherence

while still retaining the complete universal logic foundation.

---

# APPENDIX A — SOURCE PRESERVATION

The following are the exact contents of the uploaded source files, preserved without silent rewriting. This appendix is deliberately included so the master project can be audited against the original attachments.

## A1. gaming logics.txt — verbatim

```text
listing  all with complete tables waht wecan make to achieve from whole these list and make sure to add your own finding of games that we can adapt into this sect of gaming logics ."taking all the rules nd regulatins and differents modes of game play ,algorithms,with all possible probable out comes with mathematical probability is using with all permutation and combination to make game with all these games logic and designs of all possible games and startergy is whole taken with deep reserch and integrating with our game 100 % integrity of covering whole logics make sure not miss any.--"Magic square 
Sudoko
Chess
Solotaire
Uno
Tetris
Kabaddi palakka
Bingo
Playing cards all games 




Pokemon go""

Yes. The right way to approach this is not merely to combine the named games, but to build a universal game-logic engine in which the mechanics of those games become reusable modules.

Your starting set contains several fundamentally different mathematical/game-design families:

Magic Square → constraint satisfaction, numerical placement, symmetry
Sudoku → constraint satisfaction, deduction, search
Chess → deterministic strategy, state-space search, tactics, positional evaluation
Solitaire → hidden information, sequencing, probability, resource management
UNO → card effects, matching, hand management, disruption
Tetris → spatial packing, real-time decision-making, cascading state changes
Kabaddi → territory, pursuit/evasion, timing, stamina/risk
Pallanguzhi / Mancala-type logic if "palakka" refers to that family → sowing, redistribution, capture
Bingo → random draws, pattern completion, probability
Standard playing-card games → an enormous library of trick-taking, shedding, betting, matching, memory, drafting and probability mechanics
Pokémon GO → collection, exploration, progression, location, AR, trading, PvP, PvE and multiplayer. Pokémon's official description identifies collection, training, battle and trading as its enduring core themes.

The important distinction is that 100% coverage of every game ever made is mathematically impossible to prove. There are innumerable variants and combinations, and new games can create new mechanics. What we can build is a mechanic-complete architecture designed to represent essentially any game state, rule, action, probability distribution, strategy or victory condition.

1. MASTER GAME-LOGIC ARCHITECTURE

Think of the proposed system as:

GAME = STATE + PLAYERS + RULES + ACTIONS + INFORMATION + RESOURCES + RANDOMNESS + TIME + OBJECTIVES + OUTCOME

Layer	What it controls	Examples from your games
1. Players	Number, teams, roles	Chess 2, UNO 2–10+, Kabaddi teams
2. Board	Spatial environment	Chess board, Sudoku grid, Tetris well
3. Pieces	Objects controlled	Chess pieces, Pokémon, cards
4. Deck	Ordered/random objects	UNO, Solitaire, playing cards
5. Hand	Private objects	UNO, Solitaire
6. Grid	Discrete positions	Sudoku, Magic Square, Tetris
7. Territory	Controlled area	Kabaddi, chess regions
8. Time	Turns, clocks, real-time	Chess clock, Tetris speed
9. Actions	What players can do	Move, draw, discard, attack
10. Constraints	Legal-state restrictions	Sudoku, chess
11. Resources	Limited strategic assets	Cards, stamina, lives
12. Information	Visible/hidden knowledge	Solitaire, poker
13. Randomness	Chance	Card shuffle, bingo
14. Probability	Outcome likelihood	Card draw, catch, combat
15. Progression	Increasing power/difficulty	Pokémon
16. Economy	Exchange/value	Trading, buying, upgrading
17. Combat	Conflict resolution	Chess capture, Pokémon battle
18. Collection	Acquisition	Pokémon, cards
19. Pattern	Recognizing/completing structures	Sudoku, Bingo
20. Spatial packing	Fit/placement	Tetris
21. Simulation	World/state evolution	Pokémon GO
22. Social	Cooperation/trading	Pokémon
23. AI	Computer opponents	Chess
24. Scoring	Numerical evaluation	Bingo, cards
25. Victory	Winning conditions	Checkmate, line completion
26. Failure	Losing/elimination	Chess mate, Tetris top-out
27. Meta-game	Long-term progression	Collection, ranking
28. Generation	Procedural creation	Random boards/decks/maps
29. Replayability	Variation between games	Seeds, randomization
30. Analytics	Measurement	Win rate, ELO, difficulty

Dungeons & Dragons	RPG	Dice, narrative, character development	Stochastic simulation	Hidden
Go Fish	Matching	Memory	Probability	Hidden
Poker	Betting	Bluffing, probability	Bayesian/psychological	Hidden
Bridge	Trick-taking	Bidding, partnership	Combinatorial/probability	Hidden

This is important because chess alone is not the complete mathematical model of games. Combinatorial game theory covers perfect-information games, while hidden-information and stochastic games require additional mathematical machinery. Algorithmic combinatorial game theory specifically studies algorithms, complexity and ideal play.

33. GAME VALIDATION ENGINE

Every generated game should automatically be tested.

Test	Question
Rule consistency	Are rules contradictory?
Reachability	Can states actually be reached?
Termination	Can the game become infinite?
Solvability	Can objectives be achieved?
Balance	Is one strategy dominant?
Probability	Do probabilities sum to 1?
Fairness	Are starting positions equivalent?
Exploit detection	Is there a broken strategy?
Dominant strategy	Is one action always optimal?
Deadlock	Can players become permanently stuck?
Difficulty	Is challenge appropriate?
Replayability	Are games sufficiently variable?
AI solvability	Can AI find reasonable moves?
Economy	Can resources inflate/deflate uncontrollably?
Runtime	Can the simulation execute efficiently?
34. MONTE-CARLO BALANCE TESTING

Run the game thousands/millions of times.

For example:

N=1,000,000

Then measure:

Metric	Result
Player 1 win rate	49.7%
Player 2 win rate	50.3%
Average duration	23.4 min
Draw rate	1.1%
Average score	182
Strategy A success	48%
Strategy B success	52%

Now balance becomes empirical rather than subjective.

The resulting platform isn't just an individual game.

It could become a Game Logic Laboratory / Universal Game Engine capable of creating:

Strategy games

And the physical tabletop side is also practical: modern game-design platforms already support custom boards, cards, pieces, rules and print-on-demand prototypes, demonstrating that this type of modular component approach is workable.

38. THE MOST IMPORTANT DESIGN PRINCIPLE

Don't build:

"Chess + Sudoku + UNO + Tetris + Pokémon."

Build:

A universal state-transition system capable of expressing Chess, Sudoku, UNO, Tetris, Solitaire, Bingo, card games, Kabaddi, Mancala/Pallanguzhi, Pokémon-style collection/exploration and thousands of additional mechanics as configurations.

That distinction is enormous.

The architecture should ultimately look like:

GAME=STATE+RULES+ACTIONS+INFORMATION+TIME+RANDOMNESS+OBJECTIVES+ECONOMY+STRATEGY+WORLD

The first equation handles what can happen; the second handles how likely each outcome is.

That gives us the foundation for the next stage: a Master Game Mechanics Database, where every mechanic gets a unique ID, mathematical model, state variables, inputs, outputs, legal conditions, probability model, AI algorithm, victory interaction, balance parameters and compatibility with every other mechanic. This is the level at which we can systematically pursue the "don't miss any logic" requirement rather than simply making a long list of games.

make sure the algorithm and patterns in all game we mentioned and you added are taken into consideration with deep context of new game building

Absolutely. I would strengthen the earlier architecture substantially.

The goal should not be to take the surface rules of Chess, Sudoku, Tetris, UNO, etc. and mash them together. We should extract their underlying algorithms, recurring patterns, decision structures, mathematical constraints, information structures, strategy patterns, failure modes and progression loops, then make those primitives available to the new-game generator.

This is consistent with existing game-mechanics taxonomies: mechanics are increasingly catalogued as reusable functional systems, while research on general game playing has explicitly represented games through concepts covering properties, equipment, rules and mathematics. One published Ludii taxonomy, for example, reported 428 distinct game concepts in its implementation.

1. THE NEW TARGET: A UNIVERSAL GAME LOGIC SYSTEM

The architecture should become:

GAME=STATE+RULES+ACTIONS+PATTERNS+ALGORITHMS+INFORMATION+PROBABILITY+OBJECTIVES+TIME+STRATEGY
	​


And every game should be representable as:

F = complete rules transition function

The critical addition is PATTERNS.

A new game should not merely know that a piece moved. It should understand:

What pattern did that move create, destroy, threaten, complete, prevent, reveal or optimize?

2. FIVE LEVELS OF GAME LOGIC

Every mechanic from the games we discussed should be decomposed into five layers.

Level	Question	Example
L1 — Primitive	What happened?	Move card
L2 — Rule	Was it legal?	Card matches color
L3 — Pattern	What structure resulted?	Three-card sequence
L4 — Strategy	Why is it valuable?	Preserves future options
L5 — Outcome	What changed?	Opponent loses turn

This prevents us from copying games superficially.

3. COMPLETE ALGORITHM FAMILIES

These should become first-class modules.

Family	Algorithms / methods	Games contributing the logic
Search	BFS, DFS, iterative deepening	Chess, puzzles
Adversarial search	Minimax, alpha-beta	Chess, Checkers
Monte Carlo	Random rollout, MCTS	Go, strategy games
Constraint solving	CSP, propagation	Sudoku, Magic Square
Backtracking	Recursive search	Sudoku, puzzles
Exact cover	Algorithm X / DLX	Sudoku-type problems
Graph algorithms	BFS, DFS, Dijkstra, A*	Maps, territory
Optimization	DP, branch-and-bound	Strategy/resource games
Probability	Exact enumeration	Cards, Bingo
Bayesian reasoning	Posterior inference	Poker, Battleship
Simulation	Monte Carlo simulation	Probability games
Game theory	Nash/utility analysis	Competitive games
Scheduling	Priority/queue algorithms	Action systems
Pathfinding	A*, Dijkstra	Exploration
Matching	Bipartite/constraint matching	Cards/resources
Pattern detection	masks, sequences, adjacency	Bingo/Tetris
Spatial algorithms	collision, packing	Tetris
Physics	velocity, collision, forces	Physical games
Procedural generation	noise, grammar, constraints	RPG/world
AI learning	RL, policy/value learning	Adaptive opponents
Evolution	genetic search	Game generation
Clustering	player/game classification	Analytics
Prediction	statistical/ML models	Opponent modeling
Optimization + search	hybrid MCTS/CSP	Advanced games

MCTS is particularly valuable because it can operate on the general abstraction of state + action + simulation, rather than requiring a separate algorithm for every game.

Research is also actively combining MCTS with combinatorial optimization, which supports the architecture we are proposing rather than forcing search and constraint systems into isolated silos.

These are far more valuable to the universal engine than chess piece names.

28. THE GAME GENERATOR MUST NOT COMBINE EVERYTHING

This is critical.

If we combine every mechanic blindly, we get an incoherent game.

Instead, create compatibility rules.

For example:

Mechanic A	Mechanic B	Compatibility
Chess movement	Territory	High
Sudoku constraints	Chess combat	Medium
Tetris gravity	Poker betting	Low
Card draw	RPG loot	Very high
Hidden information	Bluffing	Very high
Real-time	Deep minimax	Difficult
Permadeath	Competitive ranking	Possible
Probability	Puzzle	Conditional
Physics	Sudoku	Usually low
Trading	Collection	Very high

So the generator needs:

Compatibility(A,B)

not merely:

A+B

We should explicitly measure this.

Conceptually:

Outcome=Skill+Luck+Interaction

For deterministic Chess:

Luck≈0

For a pure lottery:

Skill≈0

For Poker:

Skill>0,Luck>0

For your new games, we can tune a parameter:

SLR=
SkillContribution+LuckContribution
SkillContribution
	​


So a designer could request:

70% skill / 30% uncertainty

and the generator attempts to produce a game in that range.

34. PLAYER EXPERIENCE VECTOR

We should also model what the game feels like.

G=(T,C,U,R,S,P,E)

where:

Parameter	Meaning
T	Tension
C	Complexity
U	Uncertainty
R	Risk
S	Strategy depth
P	Pace
E	Emotional volatility

This is important because two games can use identical mechanics but feel completely different.

Game-design taxonomy research similarly emphasizes that games are not adequately described by isolated rules; player interaction patterns and gameplay "gestalts" matter.

36. WHAT "COMPLETE COVERAGE" SHOULD MEAN

I would change our original requirement from:

"Include every game."

to:

"Include every identifiable fundamental game-logic family, algorithmic family, information model, randomness model, pattern family, action type, resource relationship, strategic interaction and victory structure necessary to reconstruct or generate games across the identified domains."

That is a technically defensible target.

A literal list of every game cannot guarantee completeness because game-mechanics taxonomies themselves are living/expanding systems; one current database advertises over 180 mechanics and more than 10,000 games, while another taxonomy continues to evolve its mechanic vocabulary.

37. THE MOST IMPORTANT NEW ADDITION

The next version should therefore have three databases, not one.

Then the system becomes:

Knowledge→Mathematics→Mechanics→Patterns→Algorithms→Generation→Simulation→Balance→New Game
	​


That is the direction I would take for your project. It preserves the deep logic of Chess, Sudoku, Magic Square, Solitaire, UNO, Tetris, Bingo, card games, Kabaddi, Pallanguzhi/Mancala, Pokémon-style collection/exploration, Go, Minesweeper, Poker, Battleship, Mastermind, Backgammon, Risk, Catan, Cluedo, Pandemic, RPGs and the additional families—without treating them as isolated games.

The next serious step would be to turn this into a master Game Logic Ontology/Table, where every game → every mechanic → every sub-mechanic → every pattern → every algorithm → every mathematical model → every possible state transition → every compatibility relationship receives a unique ID. That would give us the actual specification from which the new-game-building engine could be designed.

Unified Architectural Synthesis of Combinatorial Games, Probabilistic Systems, and Kinetic SimulationsIntroductionThe development of a comprehensive, unified game engine that completely integrates the mechanical logic, state-space permutations, probability matrices, and game-theoretic strategies of highly disparate ludic systems represents a paramount challenge in computational architecture. Such a system must seamlessly oscillate between deterministic constraint-satisfaction problems, hyper-stochastic combinatorial card environments, continuous-space heuristic puzzles, and the geometric abstraction of physical contact sports. The challenge necessitates a rigorous deconstruction of the rules, regulations, mathematical probabilities, and underlying algorithmic frameworks for a diverse array of models.This analysis exhausts the mathematical underpinnings and programmatic logic required to integrate numerical topological constraints (Sudoku, Magic Squares), game-tree heuristic models (Chess, Tetris), finite-state permutation environments (Standard Playing Cards, Poker, Bridge, Solitaire, Uno), hypergeometric grid matrices (Bingo), geospatial augmented reality frameworks (Pokémon GO), and kinetic-to-deterministic board sport translations (Kabaddi and its board analog "Tackled"). By capturing every possible permutation and combination with absolute probabilistic precision, a grand architectural schematic is formed to guarantee complete logical integrity for the proposed meta-game system.Constraint Satisfaction and Numerical Topology FrameworksThe lowest operational layer of the integrated architecture relies on exact constraint solvers. These systems feature zero stochastic variance and are defined entirely by topological laws and matrix completeness.The Mathematics and Algorithmic Logic of SudokuSudoku is the quintessential deterministic constraint-satisfaction paradigm, fundamentally formulated as an Exact Cover problem. A standard architectural engine must generate, evaluate, and solve a $9 \times 9$ matrix such that digits 1 through 9 appear exactly once in every row, column, and designated $3 \times 3$ subgrid. The total permutation space for valid, completely filled $9 \times 9$ Sudoku grids has been computationally proven to be precisely $6,670,903,752,021,072,936,960$ ($6.67 \times 10^{21}$). However, by factoring out geometric symmetries, rotations, reflections, and digit permutations, the core algorithmic logic only needs to account for $5,472,730,538$ essentially distinct normal grids.To programmatically integrate Sudoku into the broader game engine, the system utilizes a binary matrix transformation to execute Donald Knuth’s Algorithm X, optimized via the Dancing Links (DLX) technique. This architecture translates the $9 \times 9$ grid into a massive state-space matrix of 729 rows and 324 columns. The 729 rows correspond to the total possible variable assignments (81 grid cells multiplied by the 9 possible digits per cell). The 324 columns represent the absolute constraints that the engine must perfectly satisfy without overlap. These are partitioned into four categories, each containing 81 specific requirements: cell constraints (each of the 81 cells must hold exactly one digit), row constraints (each of the 9 rows must contain all 9 digits), column constraints (each of the 9 columns must contain all 9 digits), and box constraints (each of the 9 subgrids must contain all 9 digits).The DLX algorithm addresses this Exact Cover problem through a highly efficient depth-first search on a toroidal doubly-linked list. When the heuristic engine selects a candidate row representing a specific digit placed in a specific cell, it algorithmically traverses the corresponding constraint columns and entirely excises them—along with any other rows that intersect these columns—from the active matrix. If the engine iterates to a state where the matrix is completely empty, a valid Sudoku permutation has been derived. Conversely, if a column is devoid of any intersecting rows before the matrix is empty, the logic registers a failure and initiates chronological backtracking. To incorporate gameplay difficulty, the engine sequentially removes digits from the completely solved state while continually running the DLX solver to ensure that the puzzle maintains a unique, singular solution, bounding the minimal required clues to no fewer than 17.Magic Squares: Symmetries, Parity Algorithms, and Constant GenerationMagic squares demand mathematical rigidities akin to Sudoku but shift the constraints from exclusive distinctness to topological summations. A magic square of order $n$ requires an $n \times n$ matrix populated by distinct consecutive integers from 1 to $n^2$ such that the sum of every horizontal row, vertical column, and both main diagonals equate to a singular magic constant, $M(n)$. The algebraic formula defining this constant is intrinsic to arithmetic progressions:
$$M(n) = \frac{n(n^2 + 1)}{2}$$
For the foundational order of $n=3$, the matrix necessitates a constant of $15$. The combinatorial complexity scales aggressively; while there is only 1 distinct magic square of order 3 (discounting topological rotations and reflections), the state space expands to 880 distinctly different normal squares of order 4, and escalates to $275,305,224$ unique matrices for order 5.Beyond standard magic squares, the system must accommodate advanced topological variations. Semimagic squares abandon diagonal equivalency to satisfy only row and column constants, serving as transitional mathematical objects. Panmagic (or diabolic) squares introduce a wrapping topology wherein all broken diagonals parallel to the main diagonals also equal the magic constant. Bimagic and trimagic squares represent higher-order algebraic complexities where the matrix retains its magic properties even when every integer is squared or cubed. The generation algorithms embedded in the master engine depend entirely on the integer parity of $n$.For odd-order matrices ($n = 2k + 1$), the engine employs the Siamese Method, originally documented by de la Loubère. The procedural algorithm initializes the matrix by placing the digit 1 in the exact center cell of the top row. The program then calculates the next placement vector, moving one coordinate unit diagonally upward and to the right. The grid behaves as a continuous topological torus: exceeding the top boundary wraps the vector to the bottom row, and exceeding the right boundary wraps it to the left-most column. If the algorithm encounters a spatial collision—attempting to place an integer in a previously populated cell—a break vector is triggered, redirecting the placement directly below the last placed integer, after which the diagonal vector resumes.For doubly even matrices ($n = 4m$), the engine applies a sequence masking approach. The matrix is geometrically subdivided into discrete $4 \times 4$ subgrids, and the algorithm draws theoretical 'X' marks across the main diagonals of every subgrid. The engine iterates a standard sequential array from 1 to $n^2$ through the entire grid. For coordinates intersecting the drawn 'X' paths, the sequential number is placed normally. However, for all coordinates falling into uncrossed voids, the value is computationally inverted according to the equation $(n^2 + 1) - a_{(i,j)}$.The paramount algorithmic complexity arises when solving for singly even matrices ($n = 4m + 2$). For these instances, the engine utilizes the "LUX" method authored by John Horton Conway. The foundational architecture relies on generating a smaller, abstract precursor array of dimensions $(2m + 1) \times (2m + 1)$. This array is populated downward with $2 \times 2$ block designators: $m + 1$ rows of the letter 'L', exactly 1 row of the letter 'U', and $m - 1$ rows of the letter 'X'. The core algorithm mandates an immediate topological exchange, swapping the centrally positioned 'U' character with the 'L' character directly above it. Once this precursor array is established, the engine processes it via the Siamese method, moving from letter block to letter block. Each letter dictates a specific micro-sequence for filling its constituent $2 \times 2$ matrix with four consecutive digits. The morphological design of the characters L, U, and X dictates the sequential path the digits follow within the micro-cluster, elegantly satisfying the magic constraints across the entire macro-matrix.Probabilistic Permutations and Finite State Card MechanicsThe second architectural layer departs from the rigid topological guarantees of numerical grids and enters the hyper-dimensional space of permutations and combinations. In adapting standard playing cards, Uno, and Bingo, the engine must execute exact probabilistic modeling and Finite State Machine (FSM) tracking.Standard Deck Permutations, Solitaire, and Bridge LogicsA standard French-suited playing card deck operates as a closed mathematical set of 52 discrete elements. The randomization sequence required for game initialization represents a permutation space of $52!$ (approximately $8.0658 \times 10^{67}$ possible shuffles). In the context of a four-agent game like Bridge, the exact mathematical distribution of hands among the players generates $\frac{52!}{(13!^4)}$, evaluating to $5.3645 \times 10^{28}$ possible unique dealing configurations.This state-space explosion is the primary obstacle in algorithmicizing Solitaire, specifically the prevalent Klondike variant. A computational solver evaluating a Klondike state maps seven tableau columns, four suit-specific foundation piles, a stockpile, and a waste pile. Because a pure traversal of the $52!$ game tree is computationally unfeasible, the engine's integration of Solitaire logic applies Depth-First Search (DFS) algorithms aggressively pruned by heuristic evaluation. Forced moves—such as transitioning an exposed Ace to a foundation pile—are executed without consuming processing cycles for alternative branches. Solitaire engines frequently utilize Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) methodologies to navigate the stochastic elements of unknown face-down cards, mathematically proving that approximately 80% to 82% of all initial deals are technically solvable, despite standard human completion rates resting significantly lower due to incomplete information.Poker Combinatorics and Exact Game Theory AnalysisIntegrating Poker logic—predominantly Texas Hold'em and standard five-card draw mechanics—demands absolute precision in evaluating combinations and mathematical scarcity. The engine processes the total population of unique five-card combinations drawn from a 52-card deck using the binomial coefficient $\binom{52}{5}$, which strictly evaluates to 2,598,960 potential hands.The valuation hierarchy within the game architecture maps entirely to the inverse probability of these combinations occurring. The exact mathematical derivation for every state is integrated as follows:Hand RankCombinatorial Derivation FormulaExact CombinationsProbability of OccurrenceRoyal FlushFixed deterministic outcome per suit.4$0.000154\%$Straight FlushSequences per suit minus the 4 Royal Flushes.36$0.00139\%$Four of a Kind$\binom{13}{1} \times \binom{4}{4} \times \binom{48}{1}$624$0.0240\%$Full House$\binom{13}{1} \times \binom{4}{3} \times \binom{12}{1} \times \binom{4}{2}$3,744$0.1441\%$Flush$\binom{13}{5} \times 4 - 40$5,108$0.1965\%$Straight$10 \times 4^5 - 40$10,200$0.3925\%$Three of a Kind$\binom{13}{1} \times \binom{4}{3} \times \binom{12}{2} \times 4^2$54,912$2.1128\%$Two Pair$\binom{13}{2} \times \binom{4}{2} \times \binom{4}{2} \times \binom{11}{1} \times 4$123,552$4.7539\%$One Pair$\binom{13}{1} \times \binom{4}{2} \times \binom{12}{3} \times 4^3$1,098,240$42.2569\%$High Card$(\binom{13}{5} - 10) \times (4^5 - 4)$1,302,540$50.1177\%$TotalSummation of all possible discrete outcomes.2,598,960100.0000%In game engine architecture, rather than processing conditional cascades during live gameplay to evaluate hands, these precise combinatorial equations are pre-compiled into a rapid lookup table utilizing bitwise operations, guaranteeing $O(1)$ evaluation speeds. The overarching strategy module implements Game Theory Optimal (GTO) play, seeking Nash Equilibriums where the AI balances its ranges (bluffing frequencies versus value betting) based entirely on the probabilistic equity dictated by this combinatorial matrix.Finite State Automata in UnoWhereas standard cards emphasize combination matching, the Uno ecosystem functions as a deterministic Finite State Machine perturbed by specific operator inputs. A standard deck contains 108 cards mapping to four color vectors (Red, Yellow, Green, Blue). Each vector contains one 0, pairs of digits from 1 to 9, pairs of structural action cards (Skip, Reverse, Draw Two), augmented by four Wild and four Wild Draw Four cards.The system logic models the game state through a continuous variable loop. The core variables tracked are Current_Color, Current_Value, a boolean Play_Direction flag, and the hand arrays of $N$ participating agents. A player’s submission is validated if it fulfills a logical OR condition matching the Current_Color, matching the Current_Value, or possessing the Wild property.When action cards are deployed, the engine triggers immediate interrupts within the FSM:The Reverse algorithm intercepts the sequential agent turn vector, multiplying the current turn increment by $-1 \mod N$.The Skip algorithm increments the current turn pointer by $2 \mod N$, circumventing the adjacent logical node.The Draw Two and Draw Four modules force an immediate push operation onto the targeted agent's array, appending random elements from the deck array, and subsequently invoke the Skip protocol.A significant architectural consideration in Uno programming is state entrapment. Due to the requirement to draw cards when a valid play is impossible, a mathematically possible loop exists where game length approaches infinity. The engine combats this by enforcing Markov chain models to project average completion thresholds and executing terminal conditions if the stockpile cycles beyond operational limits.Hypergeometric Distributions in BingoOperating parallel to permutations is the probability matrix of Bingo. A 75-ball variant integrates deeply with hypergeometric distributions, functioning as a sampling mechanism without replacement. The 75 balls are sequentially partitioned into five bins of 15, directly corresponding to the B, I, N, G, and O columns. A valid playing card is constructed as a $5 \times 5$ spatial matrix, featuring a central non-value "free space" at coordinate $(3,3)$.The combinatorics of generating the Bingo matrices rely on choosing distinct elements from finite sets. For the columns corresponding to B, I, G, and O, the architecture selects 5 numbers from 15, yielding $\binom{15}{5} = 3,003$ permutations per column. The central 'N' column, offset by the free space, requires selecting only 4 numbers from 15, yielding $\binom{15}{4} = 1,365$ combinations.The macro-state space of mathematically distinct 75-ball Bingo cards evaluates to a massive array:$$\binom{15}{5}^4 \times \binom{15}{4} = 3003^4 \times 1365 = 111,007,923,832,370,565$$
This guarantees $1.11 \times 10^{17}$ unique matrices. During simulation, the engine initializes every active Bingo matrix as a multi-dimensional array of bitwise flags. As integers are stochastically generated from the 1-75 pool, the system executes a bitwise AND operation across all matrices simultaneously. A dedicated evaluation script continuously parses the matrices for winning state bitmasks corresponding to horizontal lines, vertical columns, and contiguous diagonals.Heuristic Search and Continuous Space ArchitectureBridging the gap between rigid cards and dynamic sports requires intermediate logic engines that handle spatial layouts, geometric gravity, and massive decision trees. Chess and Tetris provide the foundational algorithms for these processes.Perfect Information Game Trees in ChessChess operates as a purely deterministic abstract strategy game characterized by perfect information. The physical representation maps to an $8 \times 8$ continuous grid holding 32 operational pieces. The state-space complexity is formally estimated at $10^{43}$ legal board arrangements, while the entire game tree complexity—representing the mathematical bounds of all possible sequential games (the Shannon Number)—is an astronomical $10^{120}$.Because calculating the $10^{120}$ space is physically impossible, the engine's integration of Chess mechanics relies on "Bitboards." A bitboard maps the 64 squares of the matrix into a single 64-bit integer, maintaining separate integers for white pawns, black knights, occupied squares, and empty space. Move generation uses sliding array multiplication and bitwise shifts to instantaneously project movement vectors, applying ray-casting techniques to halt sliding pieces (Bishops, Rooks, Queens) upon collision with intersecting bitboards.Strategic AI integration applies the Minimax algorithm coupled with aggressive Alpha-Beta pruning. The engine iterates down a depth-first search tree to a processing limit $D$. Alpha-Beta pruning evaluates bounding scores, immediately culling entire branches of the decision tree if mathematical proof dictates a superior alternative exists elsewhere, thereby preserving CPU resources. At the terminal nodes of depth $D$, a heuristic function analyzes the bitboard state, computing positional scores based on material count, centralized pawn structures, King safety, and space control. To emulate advanced engines, this static logic is merged with Monte Carlo Tree Search and Deep Neural Networks, substituting rigid mathematical evaluations with probability-driven policy networks that prioritize optimal continuation pathways over raw material advantages.NP-Completeness and Algorithmic Geometry in TetrisTetris shifts the spatial heuristic into a continuous time-step environment dependent on a $10 \times 20$ grid matrix and the gravitational application of seven distinct tetromino variants (I, J, L, O, S, T, Z). To successfully emulate and analyze Tetris logic, the system must acknowledge that offline Tetris, played with total foreknowledge of incoming pieces, is mathematically proven to be NP-complete. No algorithm can process an optimal survival path in polynomial time.Consequently, the programming logic relies heavily on the stochastic piece-generation array and local heuristic evaluations. A naive true-random implementation is structurally flawed as it mathematically allows for infinitely long sequences of identical pieces, guaranteeing an unwinnable state. Thus, the engine utilizes the "7-bag system." The algorithm generates an array containing the seven tetrominoes, shuffles the array, and sequentially dispenses them. A new array is generated only when the current one is entirely depleted. This probabilistic safeguard ensures a deterministic cap on piece droughts; a player will never endure more than 13 consecutive drops without receiving the critical line-clearing 'I' piece.The AI evaluation for optimal spatial placement relies on the Pierre Dellacherie algorithm or similar iterative heuristic models. For every falling piece, the engine calculates the terminal geometry of all possible lateral shifts and rotations (governed by the Super Rotation System, or SRS, which allows pieces to 'kick' off walls). It scores the resultant board state against a weighted vector: it rewards cleared lines, heavily penalizes the creation of enclosed voids ("holes"), penalizes deep vertical wells, and seeks to minimize the aggregate landing height of the topography.Kinetic Physics and Algorithmic Board Emulation: KabaddiThe apex of the integration challenge involves translating physical contact sports, governed by biomechanics and physiological endurance, into an algorithmic framework, and analyzing the transition from its field reality to its board game abstraction.The Geometric and Temporal Regulations of KabaddiTraditional Kabaddi is mathematically modeled as an alternating, zero-sum contact game. The temporal constraint mandates two distinct halves of twenty minutes each, bisected by a five-minute physiological recovery period. The spatial boundary is a flat, strictly regulated rectangular matrix divided by an absolute midline. For men's professional leagues, the parameters are $13 \text{ m}$ in width by $10 \text{ m}$ in length. The women's iteration shrinks the geometric matrix to $12 \text{ m}$ by $8 \text{ m}$. A standard team array deploys 7 active agents onto the spatial grid, drawing from a predetermined substitute pool. Advanced tournaments, such as the Yuva Kabaddi Series, apply strict age-gating algorithms to the 20-member squad: players are mathematically restricted to being under 23 years of age, with a programmed allowance for precisely 2 agents aged between 23 and 25. The substitution logic utilizes a finite "coin system," permitting a maximum of 5 standard substitutions per match; beyond this threshold, only severe medical exclusions override the system logic to allow replacements.The core kinetic loop executes on a rigid 30-second temporal clock. A singular offensive agent (the "raider") pushes past the midline into the opponent's matrix, aiming to execute physical tags against defending agents while constantly maintaining a vocal "cant". The mathematical topography of the grid alters depending on the raider's spatial coordinates:Baulk Line Requirement: Positioned precisely $3.75 \text{ m}$ from the midline in both halves. The system validates a raid only when the raider's body breaks the spatial plane and makes physical ground contact between the Baulk line and the End line, ensuring simultaneously that zero coordinate mass intersects the void between the midline and the Baulk line.Bonus Line Dynamics: A parallel line situated exactly $1 \text{ m}$ further from the Baulk line toward the End line. The engine logic conditionally activates this scoring sector only if the defending team's active agent array is $\ge 6$. If the raider crosses the Bonus line but is subsequently captured, the defensive team increments their score by 1, and the bonus point is structurally voided. Furthermore, logic rules dictate that bonus points strictly bypass the player revival queue.Lobby Expansions: The vertical flanks feature designated zones measuring $1 \text{ m}$ in width. The engine treats these grids as out-of-bounds space until physical collision (a "touch" or "struggle") registers between agents. Upon collision, the bounding box algorithms dynamically expand the valid play area to encompass these lobbies.The scoring combinatorics follow precise boolean evaluations. Every defending agent tagged prior to the raider's successful retreat is flagged as "out," granting 1 point per agent to the offensive team. If the defensive array restrains the raider until the 30-second timer expires or breath is lost, the defensive team receives 1 point, and the raider is flagged "out". Crucially, the system operates a First-In-First-Out (FIFO) revival queue; acquiring an offensive or defensive point algorithmically revives a previously eliminated friendly player, provided they enter in strict sequential order.To prevent systemic stalling, the rules penalize consecutive "empty raids" (raids where points exchanged equal 0). If the engine logs two successive empty raids, the third attempt automatically activates "do-or-die" status; the raider must alter the scoreboard or cross the bonus line, otherwise, the system defaults the raider to an "out" state. Complete board wipes, known as a "Lona," award the offensive team an additional 2 points, completely flush the "out" queues, and reset the board with all 14 agents.At the macro-league level, terminal match evaluations feed into a points distribution table based on the final score delta:Match Outcome (Score Delta)Winning Team PointsLosing Team PointsWinning Margin > 7 points60Winning Margin $\le$ 7 points51Mathematical Tie33Deterministic Emulation via "Tackled"Abstracting Kabaddi's kinetic energy into an algorithmic board game like "Tackled" or "Kabaddi palakka" requires converting physical stamina into static numeric thresholds and randomized dice vectors. This simulation intends to instill team cohesion, multi-tasking, and presence of mind without requiring a 13-meter physical arena.The board state initializes by assigning two human agents the Red and Black factions. The engine randomly deals 10 cards to each faction from a master deck of 56 Kabaddi player profiles. Out of these 10 elements, 7 are assigned numerical identities using magnetic clips and populate the starting defense coordinates, while the remaining 3 are relegated to the substitute array. Crucially, the system utilizes hidden information; the card identities remain masked from the opposition.Every agent card is mathematically defined by a dual-attribute array: $[\text{Raid Score}, \text{Tackle Score}]$. The Raid integer dictates offensive penetration capability, while the Tackle integer defines defensive restraint power. The gameplay operates on turn-based loops. The offensive faction designates a raider and places a wooden marker on a starting coordinate. The system utilizes a standard 6-sided die to generate a random movement allowance, determining the exact number of steps the raider can traverse through the grid. Following the raid sequence, the defending faction utilizes the exact same stochastic dice integer to shift their defending units into overlapping "positions of influence" around the raider's final coordinate.The terminal state of the turn is resolved through an absolute mathematical comparator:Unsuccessful Raid ($\sum \text{Tackle} > \text{Raid}$): If the combined aggregate of all Tackle integers from defending units occupying positions of influence exceeds the raider's Raid integer, the raider is eliminated, defenders hold their spatial coordinates, and the defense scores 1 point.Successful Raid ($\text{Raid} > \sum \text{Tackle}$): If the raider’s offensive integer is superior to the defensive aggregate, the raider successfully escapes. All participating defenders are placed in the "out" array. The offensive faction gains points equal to the number of eliminated defenders, triggering the revival queue.Empty Raid: If the defensive array fails to maneuver any units into positions of influence, the engine registers an empty raid. Units reset to their pre-turn vectors, no points are generated, and the empty raid counter increments toward a do-or-die state.To inject volatility into the deterministic mathematics, the board topography includes "Special Boxes." Landing on these coordinates artificially inflates the agent's $[\text{Raid}, \text{Tackle}]$ array and triggers White Action Cards, forcing specific programmatic interrupts upon the opposing faction. The board adheres to standard ALL OUT (Lona) rules and terminates after exactly 20 minutes or the initial ALL OUT instance.Geospatial Augmentation and Stochastics: Pokémon GOThe final layer of the unified architecture involves integrating real-world geographic logic and continuous stochastic catch mechanics, as modeled by Pokémon GO.The engine relies on Google's S2 Geometry framework, projecting the three-dimensional Earth onto a two-dimensional mathematical cube. This surface is subsequently subdivided into hierarchical levels of S2 cells. Spawn logic operates deterministically upon these cells (frequently utilizing Level 20 parameters), executing a Poisson Point Process algorithm that correlates the volume of stochastically generated targets with the underlying density of cellular data signals in that geographical region.When an engagement is initiated, the engine executes the Capture Rate Algorithm to determine the probabilistic outcome of the interaction. The baseline equation utilizes a modified logistic function to generate a catch probability ($P$):$$P = 1 - \left( 1 - \frac{\text{Base Capture Rate}}{2 \times \text{CPM}} \right)^{\text{Multipliers}}$$Within this formula, the Base Capture Rate (BCR) is a hardcoded numerical constant unique to the target's species. The Combat Power Multiplier (CPM) acts as a dynamic floating-point variable mapping to the internal level of the target. The "Multipliers" vector aggregates all external strategic inputs into a single integer product: this includes the item physics (a standard ball represents $1.0\times$, enhanced variations offer $1.5\times$ or $2.0\times$), trajectory physics (curveballs apply a $1.7\times$ scaling modifier), item consumption (berries), and the geometric radius of the impact matrix (yielding Excellent, Great, or Nice modifiers).Upon collision, the engine stochastically generates a floating-point number $R$ between $0$ and $1$. If $R \le P$, the catch mathematically succeeds. If it fails, the architecture immediately queries a secondary Markov chain threshold based on the target's base flee rate; if a secondary RNG check fails, the entity is algorithmically deleted from the player's geospatial instance. Gym combat systems layer upon this by implementing asynchronous tick-based DPS (Damage Per Second) calculations, comparing elemental multiplier arrays (e.g., Water hitting Fire generating a $1.6\times$ coefficient) against raw stamina statistics.Master Engine SynchronizationTo guarantee 100% logic integrity across all requested gameplay parameters, the meta-game architecture must be compartmentalized into synchronized operational layers.The Combinatorial Array Matrix: This module handles the absolute mathematical topologies. It processes the Dancing Links Algorithm X for all Sudoku constraints and pre-compiles the Conway LUX and Siamese geometric algorithms for instant Magic Square constant resolution.The Hyper-Stochastic Permutation Engine: This sub-system tracks the $52!$ card permutations, calculates $O(1)$ Poker hand evaluations utilizing binomial coefficients, manages the UNO Finite State Automata, and oversees the bitwise intersection calculations for the $1.11 \times 10^{17}$ Bingo matrices.The Heuristic Physics and Geography Layer: This advanced module merges the Alpha-Beta pruning bitboards of Chess, the 7-bag sequence generator and SRS wall-kicks of Tetris, the geospatial Poisson distributions of Pokémon GO, and the intricate, time-gated, geometric boundaries of the $13 \text{ m} \times 10 \text{ m}$ Kabaddi arena.By structurally segregating purely deterministic grids from probabilistic arrays and continuous physics simulations, the core engine achieves absolute mathematical fidelity, seamlessly converting physical inertia, probability spaces, and geometric laws into a singular, flawlessly integrated computational reality.

Math frameworks the original list under-covers

    Group theory — for permutation puzzles, legal states form a subgroup of the symmetric group SnSn​, generated by the allowed moves. Rubik's Cube: state space ≈ $4.3 \times 10^{19}$, and it's proven every scrambled state solves in ≤20 moves ("God's Number"). This is the correct model for any puzzle where moves are rearrangements, not the ad hoc search you'd use for Sudoku.
    Sprague–Grundy theorem — every impartial two-player game (no hidden info, same moves available to both players, last-to-move-wins) reduces to an equivalent single Nim-heap. Compute a Grundy value via the mex (minimum excludant) function for each position; combine independent sub-games by XOR-ing their Grundy numbers. This solves Nim-family games algebraically instead of by brute search, and partially applies to Dots and Boxes.
    Computational complexity classification — worth building into your validation engine, since it tells you how expensive an AI solver needs to be before you write it: fixed tic-tac-toe is trivial (P), Minesweeper-consistency is NP-complete, generalized Sokoban and generalized Hex are PSPACE-complete. A game engine that doesn't know this will try to brute-force solve something intractable.
    Nash equilibria / payoff matrices — needed for simultaneous-move or negotiation-heavy games (Diplomacy, hidden-role voting, any rock-paper-scissors subgame). Model as a payoff matrix; solve for mixed-strategy equilibrium where no player benefits from unilaterally changing strategy.
    Shapley value — for cooperative/coalition games (Pandemic-style co-op, alliance economies in Risk/Diplomacy), this gives a mathematically fair way to attribute credit for a shared outcome across players who contributed unequally.
    Information theory (entropy) — H(X)=−∑p(x)log⁡2p(x)H(X)=−∑p(x)log2​p(x). This is the actual engine behind optimal play in feedback-deduction games like Wordle or Mastermind: at each guess, choose the option that maximizes expected information gain across the remaining candidate set, not just the option that might be right.

One worked example, since it ties several of these together: a Wordle-style module isn't really a "matching" mechanic — it's constraint satisfaction (M-family from Sudoku) plus entropy-optimal guessing (M107). Each guess partitions the remaining word list into feedback-pattern buckets; the optimal next guess is the one whose buckets are most evenly split, i.e., highest entropy. That's a genuinely different algorithm from anything Sudoku or Chess needs, which is exactly why it earns its own module rather than being folded into "constraint engine."
```
