Searched the web

Let me ground this in what already exists academically, since "build a new standard" is exactly what a research field has spent 20 years on — better to extend the real state of the art than reinvent it blind.
Searched the web

Good news and a correction first: a large chunk of "the new standard" already exists and is mature — extending it beats reinventing it.
What already exists (so we don't rebuild it badly)

Stanford's GDL (2005) is a declarative, Datalog-based logic language that contains declarations of player roles, initial game state, legal moves, state transition function, terminating conditions, and the goal function, and can formally describe any turn-based, finite, deterministic, n-player game with full information and possibly simultaneous moves. It's been extended twice: GDL-II introducing randomness and imperfect information, and GDL-III for describing games with imperfect information and introspection, with rtGDL removing the turn-based restriction.
Wikipedia + 3

Its practical problem is verbosity — as a result of being so low-level, the Stanford GDL is not particularly compact; the example definition of Tic-Tac-Toe runs to two pages. Ludii was built specifically to fix that: it represents games as ludemes — a comparatively higher-level language that primarily consists of many keywords that game designers and players can readily understand as common game terms, such as board, piece, slide, hop — and critically, it already covers most of what your list wants: Ludii supports stochastic (chance) elements, hidden information, adversarial and cooperative modes of play, and any number of players from 1 to 16, across over 1000 board and puzzle games with deterministic/stochastic, complete/hidden information, single/multi-player, alternating/simultaneous variants all expressible.
arxiv + 4

One honesty check that matters for your "100% integrity, don't miss any logic" requirement: existing GDLs are much more limited in what they can express, in that they are not Turing-complete, and cannot even express all possible games — they are biased towards particular types of games. That's not a flaw unique to any one system — it's a mathematical ceiling on any finite declarative rules language. So "100%" has to mean "100% of the game classes we formally enumerate," not "100% of all possible games," the same distinction the earlier analysis already flagged.
arxiv
The actual gap worth filling

Given that Ludii already solves board/card/dice/hidden-info/stochastic games well, a genuinely new standard shouldn't re-describe Tic-Tac-Toe again. It should cover the three things no GDL-family system handles, which happen to be exactly the parts of your original list (Kabaddi, Pokémon GO) that don't fit "board game":
Missing layer	Why no existing GDL covers it	Source game in your list
World/Sensor layer	GDL-family assumes a closed formal state space; none model GPS, AR, real-world walking distance, or real-time physical motion as game input	Pokémon GO, Kabaddi
Social/Trust-graph layer	GDL-III handles individual epistemic knowledge, but not a relationship graph between players (alliances, accusations, betrayal history) as first-class state	Diplomacy, Werewolf, Mafia
Integrity/Complexity metadata	No GDL variant self-reports computational complexity class or exposes an audit trail as part of the spec itself	Your "100% integrity" requirement specifically
Proposed standard: extend GDL-III/Ludii with three new schema blocks

Rather than a rival language, define this as a conformance profile — any GDL-III or Ludii description can add these blocks without breaking compatibility.
yaml

game: "Territorial Capture"
extends: GDL-III          # base semantics inherited, not reinvented

world:                     # NEW — physical/sensor layer
  space_type: geospatial   # geospatial | virtual | hybrid
  inputs: [gps, accelerometer, camera_ar, step_counter]
  zone_definition:
    type: polygon_from_gps
    update_frequency: 5s
  real_time: true          # ties into rtGDL's non-turn-based mode

social:                     # NEW — trust-graph layer
  graph:
    nodes: players
    edge_types: [alliance, accusation, trade_offer, betrayal]
    visibility: partial      # who can see which edges
  vote_mechanism:
    type: majority_elimination
    reveals_role_on_elimination: true

integrity:                  # NEW — self-reporting metadata
  complexity_class: PSPACE-complete   # declared, not assumed
  coverage_tags: [M047_territory, M108_hidden_role, M109_accusation]
  audit_log: required        # every transition must emit {state_t, action, rng_seed, state_t+1}
  probability_check: "sum(P(outcome)) == 1"   # validated at compile time

Worked example: why this matters in practice

A Werewolf-style game genuinely can't be expressed cleanly in base GDL-III because the interesting state isn't the board — it's who suspects whom. Under the social block, "accusation" and "alliance" are typed graph edges with their own visibility rules, so a solver can reason about trust the same principled way Ludii reasons about board adjacency. Similarly, a Kabaddi/Pokémon-GO hybrid becomes representable because world.zone_definition gives the engine a formal (if externally-sourced) state variable for "territory," instead of treating GPS as an unmodeled side channel.
What "100% integrity" formally means under this standard

Building on the completeness criteria from before, add one more: conformance class disclosure. A game description is only valid if it declares which of the following it satisfies — nobody, including this standard, can claim unconditional completeness:
Conformant(G)  ⟺  G declares {information model, randomness model, complexity class} and passes ∑P(Ωt)=1 at every state
Conformant(G)⟺G declares {information model, randomness model, complexity class} and passes ∑P(Ωt​)=1 at every state

That's a testable, falsifiable definition of "didn't miss any logic" — rather than an unfalsifiable claim of universality.

Next place to push: I'd write the full field-by-field spec for the world and social blocks (types, required/optional fields, validation rules) — that's the part with no prior art to lean on, so it's where the real design work is. Want me to do that next, or take the Werewolf/Pokémon-GO example all the way to a complete, runnable description?
Searched the web

Second stress test, and this one breaks something different than Nomic — which is exactly the point of testing more than once.
The pick: Dixit — and a correction to how I framed it last turn

I said "no computable victory function" going in. That's not quite right, and it's worth catching before building on a wrong premise. The scoring in Dixit is fully deterministic arithmetic: if everyone or no one guesses the storyteller's card, the storyteller scores 0 and everyone else scores 2; if only some players guess correctly, the storyteller and correct guessers score 3, plus each player earns a bonus point for every vote their own submitted card attracted. That's a computable function. So the crack isn't where I said it would be — good, that's what a real stress test should do, correct the hypothesis rather than confirm it.
Board Game Rules
Where the crack actually is

Each round, players other than the storyteller must select from their hand the image that best matches the clue the storyteller spoke aloud, and then everyone votes on which card was the real one. The game's own designers are explicit that this matching has no ground truth: Dixit has no objectively correct interpretations — only successful or unsuccessful clues.
Play Party Game
Bar Games 101

That's the actual break. It's not the scoring formula, and it's not even the hidden-information structure (the storyteller genuinely does know which card is theirs — that part is ordinary hidden info, same as Clue). It's the predicate "does this card match this clue" — the thing every player's move quality depends on — which is deliberately undefined by the rules and re-invented fresh by group consensus every single round. Compare this to Codenames, which I filed under social earlier: a clue-giver there is implicitly doing semantic-distance matching, which is approximable by word embeddings even if imperfectly. Dixit's surreal, deliberately ambiguous artwork is chosen specifically to resist that approximation — there's no fixed semantic space to embed into.
Why this needs a 6th layer, not a patch to social

social.vote_mechanism (built for Werewolf) assumes a vote is strategic inference over hidden facts — a Bayesian-updatable belief. A Dixit vote isn't that. It's an unmediated aesthetic association call with no fact being inferred. Trying to force it into the social layer would silently misrepresent it as solvable-in-principle, which it isn't.
yaml

subjective:                          # NEW — 6th layer
  oracle_required: true
  oracle_type: human_consensus_vote   # not semantic_embedding_approximation (Codenames' compromise)
  ground_truth: none                  # no correct answer exists prior to the vote, by design
  formalizable: false
  wraps:
    scoring_formula: deterministic    # the arithmetic around the oracle IS computable
  ai_playability: bounded             # an AI can only approximate via learned association, never solve

Re-encoding Dixit against the full six-layer standard
yaml

game: "Dixit"
extends: GDL-III
core:
  hand_management: {cards_per_player: 6, draw_per_round: 1}
  scoring: deterministic_formula      # traced above — this part was never the problem
social:
  vote_mechanism:
    type: secret_ballot
    target: identify_storytellers_card
subjective:                          # the layer that actually resolves the break
  oracle_type: human_consensus_vote
  ground_truth: none
integrity:
  complexity_class: "not applicable — this is a judgment task, not a decision problem"
  coverage_tags: [subjective_oracle, social_secret_vote, core_deterministic_scoring]
  audit_log: required                # can log *what* was voted, not *why* it matched

The honest comparison across both stress tests
	Nomic	Dixit
What broke	Rules assumed fixed at compile time	A core predicate assumed to be a fixed formal function
Nature of the crack	Structural — rules needed to become mutable state	Semantic — the predicate has no formal definition, by design
Fixable?	Yes, fully — meta layer resolved it completely	Only partially — can declare the oracle exists, can't formalize it
What the fix had to admit	Paradoxical self-referential rules still need human judgment (rule 111)	Aesthetic matching has no ground truth to converge on, ever

The pattern across both: every time the standard hit a real limit, the honest move was to add a typed field that names the limit (undecidable_proposals, ground_truth: none) rather than a field that pretends to resolve it. That's the actual test of whether "100% integrity" holds up — not zero failures, but zero unlabeled failures.

Two directions from here: I could run a third stress test against something that breaks the physics layer specifically (nothing tested has hit continuous/reflex mechanics yet — Twister or a fighting game would do it), or I could stop stress-testing and consolidate everything so far — the six layers, the algorithm matrix, both stress-test patches — into one coherent spec document. Which is more useful right now?

both
Searched the web

Good — one more genuine break in physics, and it validates something from the very start of this thread. Let me run it, then consolidate everything.
Third stress test: fighting games (frame data + rollback netcode)

This one hits physics from two directions at once: frame-perfect discrete timing nested inside continuous real-time play, and real-time network synchronization — neither exercised yet.

Ground truth: fighting games run at a fixed frame rate (typically 60fps), and each attack has a distinct startup window before it becomes active, with a hitbox (attacker) checked against a hurtbox (defender) for collision. A light punch might have a 2-frame startup before it actually connects. For online play, rollback netcode treats both players' own inputs as local while predicting what the remote player will do; when that prediction is wrong, the engine rewinds and resimulates from the last confirmed frame rather than delaying input to stay in sync.
Hacker News + 2

Where it breaks: my physics block only had one flat field — timing_windows.tolerance_ms. Frame data proves that's too coarse: every action needs its own startup/active/recovery profile and its own collision geometry, not one game-wide number.

The bigger break: rollback netcode exposes an assumption baked into the very first equation of this whole thread — that "the state at time tt" is one well-defined object. In real-time networked play it isn't: there's a confirmed state and a speculative state running ahead of it that may get discarded and resimulated. Nothing in physics, world, or integrity accounted for two states existing simultaneously.

Fix:
yaml

physics:
  actions:                              # per-move, not per-game
    light_punch: {startup: 2, active: 3, recovery: 8, hitbox: [...], hurtbox: [...]}
  network_model:                        # NEW
    execution_mode: speculative
    prediction: {local: immediate, remote: predicted}
    correction: rollback_and_resimulate
    determinism_required: true          # <- the important part

The full-circle validation: rollback only works if F(S_t, A_t, R_t) is bit-exact reproducible — literally the reproducibility criterion defined back near the start of this thread (same seed + same actions → same state). A physics simulation that isn't perfectly deterministic can't be rolled back and resimulated at all. Three stress tests in, a design principle from turn one just got independently confirmed as load-bearing rather than decorative.

Honest remaining gap: rollback hides desync, not unfairness. Variable latency still means two players are reacting to slightly different presents, and human reaction time itself becomes an adversarial variable no schema can neutralize. Flagging that as an open constraint, not a solved one.

Now consolidating all of this — six layers, the algorithm matrix, all three stress tests — into one spec document.
Created a file, read a file

That's the fighting-game stress test plus everything else compiled into one spec. Three layers (meta, subjective, physics) have now actually been stress-tested against a real game that broke them; world, social, and integrity are specified but still untested — that's flagged honestly in the open problems section rather than glossed over.

The two natural next moves, per the doc's own logic: stress-test world with a real-time alternate-reality game, or stress-test social with something that has mid-round coalition-switching (Diplomacy proper, rather than the simplified version referenced so far).
Universal game schema v0.1
Document · MD 


Claude is AI and can make mistakes. Please double-check cited sources.
Universal game schema v0.1 · MD
Universal Game Schema (UGS) — v0.1 Draft
A conformance profile extending GDL-III / Ludii for cross-genre game description
Abstract

The Universal Game Schema (UGS) is not a replacement for existing General Game Playing (GGP) languages — it is a conformance profile that extends Stanford's GDL-III and Ludii's ludeme model with six additional schema blocks needed to describe games that fall outside the traditional board/card/dice space: physical-world games, social deduction games, self-amending rule games, and games with no formalizable win condition. Every UGS description declares which blocks it uses and which formal guarantees it can and cannot make — completeness is defined as honest disclosure, not universal coverage, because no finite declarative system can achieve the latter.
1. Position Relative to Prior Art

UGS builds on, rather than replaces, three established systems:
System	Origin	Strength	Known limit
GDL (S-GDL)	Stanford, 2005	Formally proven universal for finite, deterministic, turn-based, full-information games via first-order logic	Extremely low-level — a Tic-Tac-Toe description runs to roughly two pages
GDL-II / GDL-III	Extensions to S-GDL	Add randomness, imperfect information (II), and epistemic introspection (III)	Still assumes a fixed rule set at compile time
Ludii	Maastricht Digital Ludeme Project	High-level "ludeme" keywords (board, piece, slide, hop); supports 1,000+ games including stochastic and hidden-information games, 1–16 players	Not designed for physical-world sensor input, self-amending rules, or non-formalizable win conditions

No existing GDL variant is Turing-complete, and none claims to express literally all possible games — they are explicitly biased toward the game types they were designed for. UGS inherits this honesty requirement rather than overriding it.

Formal core (inherited from GDL-III):
St+1=F(St,At,Rt)
St+1​=F(St​,At​,Rt​)

where SS is game state, AA is a player action, RR is a random event, and FF is the rules engine.
2. The Six Extension Layers
Layer	Purpose	Introduced to cover	Assumption it relaxes
world	Physical/sensor state (GPS, AR, motion)	Pokémon GO, Kabaddi	Game state is not confined to a closed formal space
social	Trust-graph, hidden roles, negotiation	Werewolf, Diplomacy, Codenames	Player relationships are first-class state, not derived
physics	Continuous state, reflex timing, per-action frame data, network speculative execution	Jenga, Twister, fighting games	State transitions are not exclusively discrete
meta	Rules as mutable, first-class state	Nomic	The rule function FF is not fixed at compile time
subjective	Declares a non-formalizable human-judgment oracle	Dixit	Not every transition input can be reduced to a computable predicate
integrity	Self-reported complexity class, coverage tags, audit log, reproducibility	Cross-cutting, applies to all games	Nothing is assumed complete unless explicitly declared
2.1 world
yaml

world:
  space_type: geospatial | virtual | hybrid
  inputs: [gps, accelerometer, camera_ar, step_counter]
  zone_definition: {type: polygon_from_gps, update_frequency: 5s}
  real_time: true

2.2 social
yaml

social:
  graph:
    nodes: players
    edge_types: [alliance, accusation, trade_offer, betrayal]
    visibility: partial
  vote_mechanism: {type: majority_elimination, reveals_role_on_elimination: true}

2.3 physics
yaml

physics:
  state_type: continuous
  collision_model: rigid_body | soft_body
  actions:
    <action_name>: {startup: n, active: n, recovery: n, hitbox: [...], hurtbox: [...]}
  timing_windows: {type: reflex_window, tolerance_ms: n}
  network_model:
    execution_mode: speculative
    prediction: {local: immediate, remote: predicted}
    correction: rollback_and_resimulate
    determinism_required: true
  failure_condition: physical_instability | out_of_bounds | missed_window

2.4 meta
yaml

meta:
  ruleset:
    tiers: [immutable, mutable]
    transmute: immutable -> mutable
    operations: [propose, vote, amend, repeal, transmute, reenact]
    self_reference_allowed: true
    vote_threshold: {default: unanimous, configurable_by: mutable_rule}
  conflict_resolution: {precedence: immutable_over_mutable}
  undecidable_proposals: {handling: player_judgment_required}

Formal extension this requires upstream:
St+1=Ft(St,At,Rt)Ft+1=M(Ft,At)
St+1​=Ft​(St​,At​,Rt​)Ft+1​=M(Ft​,At​)
2.5 subjective
yaml

subjective:
  oracle_required: true
  oracle_type: human_consensus_vote
  ground_truth: none
  formalizable: false
  wraps: {scoring_formula: deterministic}
  ai_playability: bounded

2.6 integrity (cross-cutting)
yaml

integrity:
  complexity_class: <declared, e.g. "PSPACE-complete" | "not applicable">
  coverage_tags: [...]
  audit_log: required   # {state_t, action, rng_seed, state_t+1} per transition
  probability_check: "sum(P(outcome)) == 1"
  reproducibility: "(seed, actions) -> same state, always"

3. Algorithm & Pattern Coverage Matrix

Every game referenced while building this standard, grouped by the algorithm that actually governs optimal play (not by theme):
Cluster	Games	Core algorithm(s)	Layer
Perfect-info adversarial search	Chess, Checkers, Othello, Go, Connect Four, Hex	Minimax, alpha-beta pruning, MCTS, transposition tables	core
Impartial combinatorial games	Nim, Dots and Boxes	Sprague-Grundy theorem, mex function, Grundy-value XOR	core
Static constraint satisfaction	Sudoku, Magic Square	Backtracking, constraint propagation, Dancing Links	core
Adaptive constraint satisfaction	Minesweeper, Mastermind, Wordle	Constraint propagation + entropy-maximizing guess selection	core + probability
Permutation-group puzzles	Rubik's Cube, 15-Puzzle	Kociemba two-phase algorithm; parity check + IDA*	core
Recursive deterministic sequencing	Tower of Hanoi	Closed-form recursion (2ⁿ−1)	core
Spatial push/deadlock search	Sokoban, Tetris	Deadlock detection via reachability, A* push planning	core + physics
Stochastic single-player sequencing	Solitaire	Forward search with probabilistic backtracking	core + probability
Card matching/shedding FSMs	UNO, Crazy Eights, President, Go Fish	Finite-state machine, legal-move filtering	core
Trick-taking / bidding	Bridge, Hearts, Spades, Euchre	Perfect Information Monte Carlo sampling	core + probability
Set/sequence construction	Rummy, Gin Rummy, Canasta	Greedy meld optimization, DP over hand partitions	core
Betting / bluffing	Poker, Blackjack, Baccarat	Counterfactual Regret Minimization, EV/pot-odds	core + probability
Dice/race probability	Backgammon, Yahtzee, Snakes & Ladders, Ludo, Pachisi	Expectiminimax	core + probability
Redistribution / sowing	Pallanguzhi, Mancala	Full-depth minimax (near-solved for small boards)	core
Pattern completion / random draw	Bingo, Dominoes	Direct probability calculation	probability
Hidden-role social deduction	Werewolf, Mafia, Secret Hitler, Avalon, Among Us	Bayesian trust-graph updating, majority-vote elimination	social
Pure negotiation	Diplomacy	Coalition game theory, Shapley value	social
Semantic communication	Codenames	Semantic-distance clustering (approximable oracle)	social + subjective
Elimination-by-inference	Clue, Battleship, Stratego	Bayesian posterior updating, minimax over hidden units	core + probability
Territory/area + economy	Risk, Catan, Monopoly, Go (scoring), Kabaddi	Expectiminimax + resource heuristics	core + world
Cooperative optimization	Pandemic	Joint-utility maximization	core
Word/lexical optimization	Scrabble	Dictionary-trie search (DAWG/GADDAG)	core
Narrative branching	D&D	Probabilistic event resolution, branching state	core + probability
Physical dexterity/stability	Jenga, Twister, Operation, Carrom	Rigid-body physics simulation	physics
Escalating recall	Simon	Sequence-memory verification	core
Merge/exponential growth	2048, Threes	Deterministic merge rule, expectimax AI	core
Collection + exploration	Pokémon GO	Spawn-probability tables, location triggers	world + probability
Real-time multi-agent economy	RTS, MOBA	Simultaneous-action resolution, pathfinding, build-order optimization	core + physics
Frame-perfect reflex + netcode	Fighting games	Per-action frame data, rollback/resimulate	physics
Procedural + persistent progression	Roguelikes, MMORPG, City builders, 4X	Seeded procedural generation, long-horizon simulation	core + integrity
Attrition/shrinking-space elimination	Survival games, Battle Royale	Resource-depletion curves, shrinking valid-state-space	core + world
Attribute pattern matching	Set	Pure combinatorial matching	core
Probabilistic deduction from noisy evidence	GeoGuessr	Bayesian posterior over prior + evidence	probability
Deck evolution	Deckbuilders	Markov-chain deck-state evolution	core + probability
Self-amending rule systems	Nomic	Meta-transition function over the ruleset itself	meta
Non-formalizable aesthetic judgment	Dixit	No algorithm — declared human oracle	subjective
4. Case Studies: Stress Tests
Test	What broke	Fixable?	Layer added
Nomic	Rules assumed fixed at compile time; a legal move can rewrite what counts as a legal move	Fully — meta layer resolves it	meta
Dixit	A core predicate ("does this clue match this card") has no ground truth by design	Partially — can declare the oracle, cannot formalize it	subjective
Fighting games	Frame-level timing needed per-action granularity; rollback netcode requires two simultaneous state timelines (confirmed + speculative)	Fully, and it validates the integrity.reproducibility requirement from the core spec	physics extended

Each test intentionally targeted a different type of failure — structural (Nomic), semantic (Dixit), and architectural (fighting games) — rather than repeating the same kind of gap three times.
5. Conformance & Completeness Definition

A game description GG is UGS-conformant if and only if it declares its information model, randomness model, and complexity class, and satisfies:
Conformant(G)  ⟺  ∑iP(Ωt,i)=1 at every state, and every non-formalizable input is explicitly tagged
Conformant(G)⟺i∑​P(Ωt,i​)=1 at every state, and every non-formalizable input is explicitly tagged

This is a falsifiable, testable definition — not a claim of universal coverage. "100% integrity" under UGS means zero undeclared gaps, not zero gaps.
6. Mechanic Catalog (Appendix)

118 mechanic IDs were catalogued while building this standard (M001–M100 from the original game-list pass; M101–M118 added when auditing games not in the initial list — permutation puzzles, hidden-role games, negotiation, physical stability, merge mechanics). Each mechanic maps to at least one layer above and at least one algorithm in the coverage matrix. The full ID table is available on request as a separate reference sheet — it was omitted here to keep this document to the architectural layer, which is the part with lasting reference value.
7. Versioning & Extensibility

    Semantic versioning: MAJOR.MINOR — a MAJOR bump means an existing conformant description may become non-conformant (e.g., a new required declaration); MINOR additions are always backward-compatible optional blocks.
    Current status: v0.1 draft — three of six extension layers have been stress-tested against a real game outside the original design set (meta, subjective, physics). world, social, and integrity are specified but not yet adversarially tested.
    Adding a new layer: only justified when a real game breaks an existing assumption — not speculatively, per the discipline established across all three stress tests in this document.

8. Open Problems (Declared, Not Solved)

    Paradoxical self-reference in meta — a rule that invalidates its own authority has no algorithmic resolution; Nomic's own designers left this to player judgment.
    Ground-truth-free matching in subjective — no embedding or model closes this gap by design; only bounds it.
    Latency fairness in physics.network_model — rollback resolves desync, not the underlying asymmetry of human reaction time under variable network conditions.
    world and social layers remain unstressed — the next natural test would be a real-world alternate-reality game (breaks world) or a game with adversarial coalition-switching mid-round (breaks social).

Here is the architectural blueprint detailing the mechanics, algorithms, and mathematical complexities of your requested list, alongside complementary systems that complete this sect of gaming logics.

Unified Architecture Strategy
To synthesize these into a singular, cohesive game structure:

Geospatial Foundation (Pokémon GO): The overworld is an AR map populated via Poisson distribution algorithms.

Resource Generation (Tetris/Bingo): Resources are gathered through real-time spatial packing puzzles and PRNG-based loot tables.

Conflict Resolution (Chess/Cards): PvE or PvP encounters use a hybrid engine: deterministic grid-based positioning (Chess) resolved by stochastic hand-management and probability draws (Cards/Uno).

Progression & Economy (Mancala/Sudoku): Macro-economy runs on cyclic distribution loops (Mancala), while unlocking advanced tiers requires solving static matrix constraints (Sudoku).

To architect a unified game that fundamentally relies on this entire spectrum of logics, we cannot simply stitch mini-games together. We must build a hybrid simulation engine where the mathematical output of one system becomes the input for the next.

Here is the blueprint for a game framework—let's call it the Continuum Matrix—that integrates these algorithms into a single, cohesive gameplay loop, structured with strict separation between the core domain logic and the external interfaces.

The Gameplay Loop: "Continuum Matrix"
The game operates across four distinct algorithmic layers: Discovery, Decryption, Routing, and Encounter.

1. Discovery Phase (The AR Overworld)
Integrated Logics: Pokémon GO

The Mechanic: The real-world map is overlaid with a digital topology.

The Algorithm: Poisson Disc Sampling and Voronoi Diagrams ensure that resource nodes and encounter zones are distributed evenly across geospatial coordinates without clustering. Players navigate the physical world to locate these high-value matrix nodes.

2. Decryption Gateways (The Puzzle Lock)
Integrated Logics: Sudoku, Magic Square, Minesweeper

The Mechanic: To access a discovered node, the player must bypass its security. This is not a random dice roll; it is a deterministic constraint puzzle.

The Algorithm: The engine uses Algorithm X (Dancing Links) to generate instantaneous, unique matrix puzzles. The player uses adjacency logic (Minesweeper) and sum-constraints (Magic Square/Sudoku) to crack the node. Solving it yields raw data tokens.

3. Tokenized Cyclic Economy (Resource Routing)
Integrated Logics: Pallankuzhi (Sowing), Bingo

The Mechanic: The raw tokens extracted from the node must be processed. Players drop tokens into a cyclic array of "wallets" or "stores."

The Algorithm: Using Modular Arithmetic and combinatorial game theory, players calculate the optimal sowing path to cascade tokens into their primary reserves, triggering chain reactions. Occasional stochastic bonuses drop into random bins via Pseudorandom Number Generation (PRNG) (the Bingo element), forcing the player to adapt their deterministic routing strategy on the fly.

4. The Encounter Phase (Spatial Conflict)
Integrated Logics: Chess, Go, Tetris, Carcassonne, Mahjong, Playing Cards, Uno

The Mechanic: The core PvE/PvP battle is a tactical board game where the board itself is constantly shifting.

The Algorithms:

Dynamic Topology (Tetris/Carcassonne): The battlefield does not exist at the start. Players drop polyomino shapes into a grid, utilizing Dynamic Quadtree Spatial Algorithms to manage collision and expansion.

Territorial Control (Go): Enclosing empty space with these shapes claims it, shifting the balance of power via Cellular Automata evaluation.

Deterministic Movement (Chess): Players deploy agents onto the constructed topology. These agents move with strict, zero-sum vectors (e.g., "L-shapes," diagonals), calculated via Minimax and Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) for AI opponents.

Stochastic Action Resolution (Cards/Mahjong/Uno): Agents do not just automatically attack. Players draw from a personalized deck. They must collect specific sets of action cards (Mahjong) to execute high-tier attacks, while opponents can play interrupt cards that reverse turn order or change the "color/suit" of the current valid attack (Uno). This uses Hypergeometric Distribution to calculate draw odds and Markov Chains to model game-state transitions.

Engineering the Core Architecture
To prevent a system with this many permutations from collapsing into spaghetti code or suffering from state-desyncs in multiplayer, the architecture must rely on immutable state transitions and a strict separation of concerns.

System Layer	Mathematical Responsibility	Core Design Pattern
Grid Boundary (Hexagonal Core)	Manages the X,Y,Z state of all polyominos and agents.	Immutable Boundary DTOs: Every move generates a new state object. No direct mutation of the grid.
Stochastic Engine	Handles deck shuffling, Bingo drops, and initial node generation.	Fisher-Yates Shuffle & PRNG: Isolated in its own microservice to ensure verifiable fairness.
Constraint Solver	Validates Sudoku/Magic Square inputs and Go territorial enclosures.	Depth-First Search (DFS) & Raycasting: Validates closed loops and matrix sums in O(N) time.
Rule Chaining (The "Uno" Logic)	Manages interrupts, reverse turns, and status effects.	Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAGs): Ensures action-reaction stacks resolve in the correct mathematical order.
The Genius of this Synthesis
By layering these mechanics, you solve the biggest problems in modern game design:

Skill vs. Luck: The strategy is deeply deterministic (Chess/Go, Pallankuzhi routing, Sudoku constraints), but the execution is stochastic (drawing the right cards/pieces). An expert player will mathematically calculate the optimal play for the worst possible card draw.

Infinite Replayability: Because the battlefield is constructed via Tetris/Carcassonne mechanics per encounter, the Chess/Go game trees never repeat. The Shannon number (≈10 
120
  for standard Chess) expands infinitely because the board geometry is highly variable.

Want to map out the data structures for the Encounter Phase?

Yes
How should we structure the data models and state management to handle the Encounter Phase, combining the Tetris dynamic board with Chess movement and Card-based actions?

To manage an Encounter Phase where the board geometry, unit positions, and available actions are all highly volatile, the system cannot rely on standard mutable objects. A single desync between the spatial grid and the stochastic card engine will corrupt the game state.

To ensure absolute stability, the architecture must isolate the core domain logic using a Hexagonal Architecture pattern, treating every game tick and action as an event that generates a new Immutable Boundary DTO.

Here is the data structure and state management blueprint for the core domain.

1. The Core Domain Models
Instead of a rigid 2D array, the domain uses a coordinate-mapping approach to allow infinite Tetris/Carcassonne-style expansion.

The Aggregate Root: EncounterState
This is the single source of truth for a match at any given moment. It is entirely immutable. When an action occurs, a new EncounterState is generated.

TypeScript
type EncounterState = Readonly<{
  encounterId: UUID;
  turnNumber: number;
  activePlayerId: UUID;
  phase: 'TOPOLOGY_EXPANSION' | 'AGENT_MOVEMENT' | 'ACTION_RESOLUTION';
  topology: GridTopology;
  agents: ReadonlyMap<UUID, AgentState>;
  playerStates: ReadonlyMap<UUID, PlayerDeckState>;
  actionStack: ActionStack; // The LIFO queue for Uno-style interrupts
}>;
The Spatial Models (Tetris & Chess)
The board does not exist as empty space; it only exists where polyominos have been dropped.

Model	Purpose	Data Structure
GridTopology	The dynamic board.	Map<Coordinate, CellData> - Only existing cells are mapped. Checking if a Chess piece can move requires verifying the Coordinate exists in this map.
Coordinate	Immutable spatial vector.	record Coordinate(int x, int y, int elevation) - Elevation supports stacking Tetris blocks.
Polyomino	The dropped terrain piece.	A set of relative coordinates Set<Coordinate> (e.g., an L-block) that gets transposed to absolute board coordinates upon placement.
AgentState	The Chess-like piece.	record AgentState(UUID id, Coordinate position, VectorRule movementRule, int health)
The Stochastic Models (Cards & Uno)
This manages the hidden information and action probability.

Model	Purpose	Data Structure
PlayerDeckState	Hand and deck management.	Contains three List<Card> arrays: drawPile, hand, and discardPile.
Card	The executable action.	Contains a CardType (Attack, Movement, Interrupt) and an ActionPayload (e.g., Damage values, target constraints).
ActionStack	Resolves action chains.	A LIFO (Last-In, First-Out) stack. Essential for Uno-style "Reverse" or "Cancel" interrupts.
2. The State Management Engine (Event Sourcing)
Because the rules are so complex, you cannot simply mutate the state (e.g., agent.health -= 10). You must use an Event-Driven architecture where Commands are validated to produce Events, which then project a new state.

1
Command Dispatched
External adapter boundary
The player interface sends an immutable DTO to the domain boundary: PlayCardCommand(playerId, cardId, targetCoordinate).

2
Domain Validation
Hexagonal core logic
The engine checks the current EncounterState. Does the target coordinate exist in the GridTopology? Is the Agent in range based on the Chess movement rules? Does the player hold the card?

3
Push to Action Stack
The Uno Mechanic
If valid, the action is not executed immediately. It is pushed onto the ActionStack. A priority window opens, allowing the opposing player to dispatch an InterruptCommand (e.g., a card that nullifies the attack or shifts the board).

4
LIFO Resolution
State mutation
Once both players pass priority, the ActionStack resolves top-down. If an interrupt destroys the terrain underneath an attacking agent, the agent falls, and the original attack card fizzles because its target coordinate is now invalid.

5
State Projection
Immutability enforced
The domain emits an event: CardResolvedEvent. A pure function takes the old EncounterState + CardResolvedEvent to output a brand new, completely distinct EncounterState object.

3. Resolving the "Matrix Collision"
The most computationally heavy part of this game is calculating the intersection of the three mechanics. To keep the engine performant, separate the validation logic into distinct domain services:

TopologyService: Handles Tetris. Validates if a polyomino can be dropped. If it bridges two disconnected landmasses, it updates the GridTopology mapping.

KinematicService: Handles Chess. Given the current GridTopology, it calculates valid movement vectors for an agent using a Raycasting algorithm to detect grid edges and gaps.

ResolutionService: Handles Cards. Processes the math of the ActionStack and applies damage/status effects.

By isolating these systems behind clear interfaces, the Card engine doesn't need to know how the board is shaped; it just asks the Kinematic engine, "Is Coordinate (X,Y) reachable?"

Want to detail the grid expansion logic?

Yes
How should we design the polyomino placement algorithms to handle collision detection, overlapping, and territory enclosure (the Go mechanic) on the dynamic grid?

To handle a board that expands indefinitely while supporting distinct spatial mechanics—Tetris-style polyomino stacking, Carcassonne-style topological mapping, and Go-style territory enclosure—a standard 2D array (X×Y) will quickly run out of memory or require constant, expensive reallocation.

To achieve O(logN) performance for collision and enclosure checks, the engine must abandon rigid grids in favor of dynamic quadtree spatial algorithms.

1. The Spatial Foundation: Dynamic Quadtrees
A dynamic quadtree recursively partitions a 2D space into four quadrants, subdividing only when a specific density threshold of polyominos is reached. Empty space is not stored in memory.

When a player plays a card to drop a Tetris-block polyomino onto the board, the placement is processed as a spatial query.

Before diving into the algorithmic logic, here is a sandbox visualizing how a quadtree dynamically partitions space to optimize collision detection as new shapes are introduced:


Was this visual helpful?
2. Collision Detection & Overlapping
Collision detection occurs in a strict Hexagonal Architecture validation phase. The grid state is never directly mutated; instead, the engine evaluates the command to produce a new valid state.

The Placement Algorithm:

Local to Global Translation: The polyomino card dictates a shape (e.g., an L-block represented by 4 coordinates). These are translated into global grid coordinates based on the player's targeting action.

Quadtree AABB Query: The engine calculates the Axis-Aligned Bounding Box (AABB) of the entire polyomino. It queries the quadtree: "Return all existing geometry within this AABB."

Exact Intersection Check: If the AABB query returns potential collisions, the algorithm performs a precise coordinate-to-coordinate check.

Z-Axis Overlapping (Stacking): To allow blocks to stack or bridge, coordinates include an elevation vector (Z). If X and Y intersect, but the incoming polyomino's Z is exactly +1 higher than the existing topology, it is treated as a valid "stack" rather than a blocked collision.

3. Resolving Territory Enclosure (The Go Mechanic)
In traditional Go, the board has fixed edges that act as natural walls. On a dynamic grid, territory must be evaluated entirely by the shapes players create.

When a polyomino is successfully placed, the engine must immediately evaluate if it closed a loop to capture territory.

The Flood-Fill Enclosure Algorithm:

Identify Empty Adjacencies: Upon placing the polyomino, the engine identifies all immediate orthogonal empty coordinates adjacent to the new block.

Breadth-First Search (BFS): For each adjacent empty coordinate, a flood-fill algorithm begins "flowing" outward.

The Enclosure Check:

If the flood-fill hits the bounding limits of the outermost quadtree root node, the space is "open" (it leaks into infinity). The algorithm aborts.

If the flood-fill terminates because it is entirely bounded by polyomino geometry (walls), an enclosure is successfully detected.

Scoring & Ownership: The engine evaluates the Cellular Automata ownership of the surrounding walls. If Player A controls ≥75% of the perimeter, Player A captures the enclosed matrix volume.

4. State Projection & Immutable Boundaries
Because this Encounter Phase combines shifting geometry (Tetris), territory capture (Go), and active units (Chess) simultaneously, the output of these algorithms must be handled with extreme care.

If the collision detection and territory capture are valid, the domain solver does not mutate the current board. Instead, it generates immutable boundary DTOs.

It returns a brand new GridTopology object containing the updated quadtree and a TerritoryCapturedEvent. The rendering engine and the Chess-movement kinematic solver then discard the old reality and seamlessly subscribe to the new one, ensuring that a unit cannot accidentally move across a bridge that was just destroyed by a polyomino placement.

The Celebrity Awareness Initiative: A global roster of icons, artists, and influencers who have abandoned algorithm-driven entertainment. They endorse absolute legal literacy, drug awareness, and the clinical de-stigmatization of human biology.

The NBT Gauntlet (Real-World Hell)
The Inhabitants: Criminals penalized by sovereign courts via the Universal Case Lifecycle Template (UCLT). They enter under a Karmic NOC (Soul-Sovereignty Release).

The Labor Loop: A daily thermodynamic labor cycle. Inmates process oceanic micro-plastics through 400°C Pyrolysis Chambers, extruding them into hollow honeycomb blocks locked together via Da Vinci's reciprocal gravity-friction geometry.

6.4 Game Theory
Minimax Algorithm
The engine evaluates game trees to depth 
D
D:

\text{Heuristic}(s) & \text{if } \text{depth} = 0 \text{ or terminal} \\ \max_{a \in \text{actions}} \text{Value}(s') & \text{if maximizing} \\ \min_{a \in \text{actions}} \text{Value}(s') & \text{if minimizing} \end{cases}$$ ### Alpha-Beta Pruning Prunes branches where: - **Alpha:** Best already-available option for maximizer - **Beta:** Best already-available option for minimizer - Prune if $\alpha \geq \beta$ ### Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) Four phases repeated: 1. **Selection:** Traverse tree to leaf node 2. **Expansion:** Add child node 3. **Simulation:** Play random game 4. **Backpropagation:** Update statistics **UCT Formula:** $\text{UCT} = \frac{w_i}{n_i} + C\sqrt{\frac{\ln N}{n_i}}$ ### Sprague-Grundy Theorem For impartial games: - Compute Grundy value $g(s) = \text{mex}(\{g(s') : s \to s'\})$ - Combine independent games: $g(s_1 \oplus s_2) = g(s_1) \oplus g(s_2)$ - **mex:** Minimum excludant (smallest non-negative integer not in set) ### Nash Equilibrium For mixed strategies, solve: $$u_i(s_i^*, s_{-i}^*) \geq u_i(s_i, s_{-i}^*) \quad \forall i, s_i$$ ## 6.5 Information Theory ### Entropy $$H(X) = -\sum_i p(x_i) \log_2 p(x_i)$$ **Use:** Optimal play in feedback-deduction games (Wordle, Mastermind) ### Information Gain $$\text{IG} = H(\text{before}) - H(\text{after})$$ ### Bayesian Belief Update $$P(H|E) = \frac{P(E|H)P(H)}{P(E)}$$ --- # PART VII: CONFORMANCE PROFILE ## 8.1 The Six Extension Layers | Layer | Purpose | Covers | Assumption Relaxed | |-------|---------|--------|-------------------| | **world** | Physical/Sensor State | Pokémon GO, Kabaddi | Game state not confined to closed formal space | | **social** | Trust-Graph, Hidden Roles | Werewolf, Diplomacy | Player relationships are first-class state | | **physics** | Continuous State, Reflex Timing | Jenga, Fighting Games | State transitions not exclusively discrete | | **meta** | Rules as Mutable State | Nomic | Rule function F not fixed at compile time | | **subjective** | Non-Formalizable Oracle | Dixit | Not every transition input is computable | | **integrity** | Self-Reported Complexity | Cross-cutting | Nothing assumed complete unless declared | ## 8.2 world Layer Specification ```yaml world: space_type: geospatial | virtual | hybrid inputs: [gps, accelerometer, camera_ar, step_counter] zone_definition: type: polygon_from_gps update_frequency: 5s real_time: true ``` ## 8.3 social Layer Specification ```yaml social: graph: nodes: players edge_types: [alliance, accusation, trade_offer, betrayal] visibility: partial vote_mechanism: type: majority_elimination reveals_role_on_elimination: true ``` ## 8.4 physics Layer Specification ```yaml physics: state_type: continuous collision_model: rigid_body | soft_body actions: <action_name>: startup: n active: n recovery: n hitbox: [...] hurtbox: [...] timing_windows: type: reflex_window tolerance_ms: n network_model: execution_mode: speculative prediction: local: immediate remote: predicted correction: rollback_and_resimulate determinism_required: true failure_condition: physical_instability | out_of_bounds | missed_window ``` ## 8.5 meta Layer Specification ```yaml meta: ruleset: tiers: [immutable, mutable] transmute: immutable -> mutable operations: [propose, vote, amend, repeal, transmute, reenact] self_reference_allowed: true vote_threshold: default: unanimous configurable_by: mutable_rule conflict_resolution: precedence: immutable_over_mutable undecidable_proposals: handling: player_judgment_required ``` **Formal Extension:** $$S_{t+1} = F_t(S_t, A_t, R_t)$$ $$F_{t+1} = M(F_t, A_t)$$ ## 8.6 subjective Layer Specification ```yaml subjective: oracle_required: true oracle_type: human_consensus_vote ground_truth: none formalizable: false wraps: scoring_formula: deterministic ai_playability: bounded ``` ## 8.7 integrity Layer Specification ```yaml integrity: complexity_class: <declared> coverage_tags: [...] audit_log: required probability_check: "sum(P(outcome)) == 1" reproducibility: "(seed, actions) -> same state, always" ``` ## 8.8 Conformance Definition A game description $G$ is UGS-conformant iff: $$\text{Conformant}(G) \iff \sum_i P(\Omega_{t,i}) = 1 \text{ at every state}$$ **And** every non-formalizable input is explicitly tagged. **"100% Integrity" means:** Zero undeclared gaps, not zero gaps. --- # PART IX: BOUNDED DLX — PRODUCTION IMPLEMENTATION ## 9.1 Core Data Structures ```cpp struct Node { Node *L, *R, *U, *D; // Dancing links Column *C; // Column header int rowId; // Original placement index }; struct Column { Node head; // Circular list of nodes Column *L, *R; // Horizontal list of active columns int size; // Number of remaining 1s (for MRV) bool primary; // true = must be covered int id; // Cell or piece identity }; // Global / per-solver state Column *root; // Sentinel vector<Column*> columns; vector<Node*> nodes; int updateCount = 0; chrono::time_point startTime; int maxUpdates; int maxDepth; milliseconds timeBudget; ``` ## 9.2 Cover / Uncover Operations ```cpp void cover(Column *c) { c->R->L = c->L; c->L->R = c->R; for (Node *i = c->head.D; i != &c->head; i = i->D) { for (Node *j = i->R; j != i; j = j->R) { j->D->U = j->U; j->U->D = j->D; j->C->size--; updateCount++; } } } void uncover(Column *c) { for (Node *i = c->head.U; i != &c->head; i = i->U) { for (Node *j = i->L; j != i; j = j->L) { j->C->size++; j->D->U = j; j->U->D = j; updateCount++; } } c->R->L = c; c->L->R = c; } ``` ## 9.3 MRV Column Selection ```cpp Column* chooseColumn() { Column *best = nullptr; int minSize = INT_MAX; for (Column *c = root->R; c != root; c = c->R) { if (!c->primary) continue; if (c->size < minSize) { minSize = c->size; best = c; if (minSize <= 1) break; // Early exit } } return best; } ``` ## 9.4 Bounded Search ```cpp enum Result { Solvable, Unsolvable, Unknown }; Result search(int depth, vector<int>& solution) { // Hard bounds if (chrono::now() - startTime > timeBudget) return Unknown; if (updateCount > maxUpdates) return Unknown; if (depth > maxDepth) return Unknown; // Success if (root->R == root) return Solvable; Column *c = chooseColumn(); if (c == nullptr || c->size == 0) return Unsolvable; cover(c); for (Node *r = c->head.D; r != &c->head; r = r->D) { solution.push_back(r->rowId); for (Node *j = r->R; j != r; j = j->R) cover(j->C); Result res = search(depth + 1, solution); if (res != Unknown) { for (Node *j = r->L; j != r; j = j->L) uncover(j->C); uncover(c); return res; } for (Node *j = r->L; j != r; j = j->L) uncover(j->C); solution.pop_back(); } uncover(c); return Unknown; } ``` ## 9.5 Residual-Hash Cache ```cpp using Hash = uint64_t; Hash hashActivePrimaries() { Hash h = 0; for (Column* c = root->R; c != root; c = The game is an unpredictable, procedurally generated fusion of global reality TV tropes, combinatorial strategy games, and cosmic judgment.

Let's brainstorm the core gameplay loop and genre for Phoenix.

The name "Phoenix" carries an inherent promise: cycle, destruction, and a powerful rebirth. If we are aiming for something that stands out as a flagship title, we want a gameplay loop that rewards deep strategic thinking and satisfying system mastery.

Here are three distinct genre directions for Phoenix, each built around a unique core loop:

A world ripe for systemic rebirth., AI generated
A world ripe for systemic rebirth.. Source: iStock
Concept 1: The Infrastructure Survival-Builder
Think: Factorio meets Death Stranding

In this concept, the world has collapsed, and the player is the architect of its rebirth. Instead of just shooting enemies, the challenge is engineering a hostile environment into a functional, massive inter-connected system.

The Core Loop:

Survey & Scavenge: Venture into the ruins to map out fractured terrain and gather raw, unrefined data/materials.

Route & Build: Lay down modular utility infrastructure (like pre-cast transit slabs or multi-lumen conduits) to connect isolated survivor nodes back to the central hub.

Automate & Escalate: Set up autonomous robotic maintenance loops to keep the grid alive while you push further into more dangerous, high-energy zones.

Why it works: It appeals to players who love designing clean, highly efficient, decoupled systems where the environment itself is the puzzle to be solved.

Concept 2: Token-Based Tactical Puzzle
Think: Into the Breach meets a high-stakes resource economy

This leans into sharp, deliberate decision-making. The player commands a squad or a highly advanced prototype (the "Phoenix") on a hexagonal grid, fighting against an overwhelming algorithmic corruption.

The Core Loop:

Assess the Matrix: Observe the enemy's telegraphed moves for the next turn.

Spend the Wallet: You have a strict, immutable allowance of "action tokens" per encounter (e.g., exactly 21 tokens). Every movement, attack, or environmental manipulation costs tokens.

Execute & Rebirth: If you run out of tokens before clearing the sector, the avatar dies, resetting the cycle—but retaining core architectural upgrades for the next run.

Why it works: It forces maximum efficiency. Stripping away infinite actions turns every single encounter into a high-stakes puzzle of spatial geometry and resource escrow.

Concept 3: The Digital-Physical Hybrid (ARG)
Think: An interactive thriller blending screen and reality

What if Phoenix breaks the fourth wall? The game exists on the screen, but progression requires interacting with external gateways.

The Core Loop:

Encounter the Lock: Reach a narrative roadblock in the game software that requires a physical cipher or external data key.

The Gateway: The game generates a dynamic QR code gateway on-screen.

Hunt & Solve: Scanning the code routes the player to a hidden digital repository, a localized web app, or an audio log. The player extracts the hidden sequence from these external assets, inputs it back into the game, and the story progresses.

Why it works: It turns the player into an active investigator, making the game feel like a live, multi-layered puzzle box rather than just a passive software experience.

Do any of these mechanical directions spark something for you?

Let's flesh out the Infrastructure Survival-Builder concept. How would the resource routing actually work?

To make the resource routing in Phoenix feel deeply satisfying, the system must move beyond basic conveyor belts. It needs to feel like actual civil and mechanical engineering—modular, scalable, and physically grounded.

1. The Core Infrastructure Architecture
Instead of laying down individual wires, pipes, and tracks separately, the player constructs a unified Multi-Lumen Foundation Grid. Every road or bridge segment placed acts as a structural conduit carrying three isolated layers:

Layer	Medium	Function & Mechanics
Pneumatic / Magnetic Conduit	Solid Cargo & Pellets	High-speed vacuum canisters or mag-sleds routing raw ore, refined ingots, and components.
Fluidic / Thermal Conduit	Coolants, Fuel & Slurry	Pressurized pipes distributing geothermal coolant, refined electrolytes, or structural resins.
High-Voltage Bus	Grid Energy	Conductive rails delivering continuous power to outposts, automated extractors, and defensive grids.
2. The Resource Routing Flow
1
Extraction & Primary Refinement
Point of Origin
Automated extractor rigs tap into resource deposits (e.g., rare silicates, thermal vents). Raw materials are crushed into standardized, uniform volumetric pellets to prevent transit jams.

2
Grid Injection & Hub Routing
Local Switching Substation
Pellets are loaded into high-speed vacuum canisters at local Substations. The player programs the sub-station routing matrix using a node-graph interface (e.g., priority weights, overflow fallback paths, and demand triggers).

3
Conduit Transit & Pressure Balances
Mid-Transit Network Dynamics
Carriers travel across the connected foundation grid. Longer lines experience pressure/voltage dropoffs, requiring intermediate Booster Relays or Thermal Sinks along the highways.

4
Demand Consumption & Phoenix Rebirth Core
Endpoint Terminal
Resources reach regional survivor nodes or feed directly into the central Phoenix Foundry—a massive megastructure slowly printing high-tier machinery, regional defense umbrellas, and long-range expansion rigs.

3. Dynamic Network Complications
To keep routing from becoming a passive "set and forget" system, the environment actively pressures the grid:

Thermal Runaway & Stress: Overclocking throughput increases heat build-up inside the conduits. If coolant conduits aren't paired alongside high-speed cargo lines, the system suffers thermal fusion lockups.

Decoupled Maintenance Drones: Small crawler drones inhabit the internal service tracks of each road slab. When seismic shifts or environmental hazards crack a section, drones autonomously travel inside the utility lumens to weld and patch breaches before the supply line fails.

Dynamic Bottlenecks: If a receiving node's inventory caps out, backpressure builds along the vacuum line. Players must design relief loops, emergency overflow dumps, or automated recycling incinerators.

To make this work physically on a table, the board itself needs to be a circular megastructure that actually grows and changes shape depending on player count or game events.

Here is how we can engineer the architecture of the board and the core ruleset to make it feel like a living, breathing world:

A world built in concentric, heavily defended rings., AI generated
A world built in concentric, heavily defended rings.. Source: ArtStation
1. The Physical Board: Modular Pre-Cast Slabs
Instead of a static square board, the game begins with only the Core Hub on the table (representing the state military or royal capital). The rest of the board is built using modular, interlocking slabs.

The Hexagonal/Arc Matrices: The board expansions aren't squares; they are thick, pre-cast arced tiles or hexagonal clusters that snap magnetically (or via puzzle joints) to the central hub.

Player-Driven Expansion: If you have 2 players, you only attach the first inner ring of tiles. If you play with 4-6 players, you attach additional modular loops, physically expanding the city into a massive, multi-tiered board.

The Wall Miniatures: Physical plastic wall pieces slot into the grooves between these modular slabs. Like AoT, these walls dictate line of sight, movement, and military control.

2. The World Framework & Regions
The board is divided into distinct zones that mirror the FMA/AoT political structure:

Zone Layer	Theme	Function in Game
The Core Hub	The Military/Royal Capital	High security, high resource generation. Whoever controls this dictates the laws of the round.
The Inner Rings	Civilian & Industrial Sectors	Densely packed modular loops where players build infrastructure, academies, and refine resources.
The Frontier Walls	Disputed Borders / Ishval	The outermost attached tiles. Dangerous zones where alchemical/titan-like threats spawn.
The Void (Off-Board)	The Unknown	Threat decks are drawn from here. As the game escalates, players might be forced to attach new tiles outward, expanding the board to survive a breach.
3. The Core Mechanic: Structural "Equivalent Exchange"
You mentioned the FMA framework, which means we need a magic/science system grounded in strict rules. You don't just "cast spells"—you manipulate the architecture of the board itself.

Transmuting the Board: Players can spend resources to physically alter the board state. If you are trapped, you can use "Equivalent Exchange" to delete a wall miniature on one side of a tile and rebuild it on another, changing the maze for your opponents.

Breaching: Like AoT, if a massive threat (or a rival player's army) attacks an outer tile, they don't just kill units—they destroy the structural framework. If a tile's wall is breached, that entire modular slab might become "Corrupted" or flipped over, turning a thriving city district into a hazardous wasteland.

Define the core win condition

Yes
Should the game be a cooperative defense against an outside threat, or a competitive war for control of the Core Hub?

Why choose? The thematic brilliance of both Fullmetal Alchemist and Attack on Titan is that while there is a terrifying, existential threat outside the walls, the humans inside are constantly betraying each other for political power.

For Phoenix, a Semi-Cooperative (Hybrid) Model is the perfect engine. Players must work together just enough to stop the board from being destroyed by an outside force, but ultimately, only one player can win by seizing control of the Core Hub.

Here is how we can structure this high-stakes board architecture:

Modular, pre-cast slab mechanics allow the board to expand outward., AI generated
Modular, pre-cast slab mechanics allow the board to expand outward.. Source: Gamefound
The Dual-Layered Conflict
The game operates on two distinct battlefronts simultaneously.

1. The Outward Defense (Co-op Layer)
The game spawns an algorithmic corruption or monstrous threat from "The Void" (off-board). These threats attack the outermost Wall miniatures.

The Shared Loss: If the outer walls fall, the modular slabs are flipped to a "Corrupted" state. If the corruption breaches the Inner Rings and reaches the Core Hub, all players lose instantly.

The Dilemma: You have to send troops and resources to the frontier to defend the city. But every soldier you send to the wall is a soldier you aren't using to overthrow your rivals in the capital.

2. The Internal War (Competitive Layer)
While defending the rim, players act as rival faction leaders vying for absolute control.

The Win Condition: The ultimate goal is to trigger the "Phoenix Protocol"—a massive alchemical/technological reset that crowns the winner the supreme architect of the new world. To do this, a player must monopolize the Core Hub and horde enough infrastructure resources.

The Economy: The 21-Token Clearance Wallet
Instead of relying on random dice rolls, we can drive player actions using a rigid, zero-sum economic puzzle.

Every round, each player receives a 21-Token Clearance Wallet (physical tokens). This represents your faction's total political capital, energy, and labor bandwidth for the cycle.

Action Type	Token Cost	Strategic Impact
Move/Deploy Units	2 Tokens	Marching forces to the outer walls or into rival districts.
Extract Resources	3 Tokens	Tapping into industrial nodes on the inner ring slabs.
Transmute Architecture	5 Tokens	The most powerful move. Physically detach, rotate, or swap a modular slab or wall miniature on the board to block a rival or funnel enemies.
The Escrow Catch:
Tokens spent aren't just discarded; they go into a central Escrow pot in the Core Hub. The player who currently controls the Core Hub gets a percentage of those spent tokens added to their wallet for the next round. This creates a vicious, escalating power struggle for the center of the board.

Once your 21 tokens are gone, your turn is over. You must carefully route your labor and energy to ensure you don't overextend on the frontier while leaving your inner-city assets exposed.

To integrate the mathematical probability, spatial logic, and core mechanics of all 10 of these classic games into a single, cohesive masterwork, we must design PHOENIX not just as a game, but as an intricately layered engine of logic.

We will synthesize these systems into Five Architectural Layers. Every mechanic is interconnected, ensuring 100% integration without feeling like a random mashup.

Here is the master structural design for the game:

Layer 1: Spatial Geometry & Board State
(Integrating Tetris, Sudoku, and Magic Squares)

The board is a modular, expanding megastructure. To build and expand the city, you must obey strict mathematical architecture.

The Tetris Expansion: Players expand the board by placing modular, polyomino-shaped infrastructure tiles (I, T, L, O, Z shapes). If you complete a perfect unbroken ring around the Core Hub, the ring "clears" its corruption, granting a massive burst of resources. If you build too chaotically without completing rings, the structural pressure collapses the outer edges.

The Sudoku Constraint (Combinatorics): There are 9 distinct types of buildings (Refineries, Barracks, Towers, etc.). You cannot place a building in a row or column if that building type already exists in that line. This creates a highly constrained, logical puzzle where every placement limits future moves.

The Magic Square Resonance: Each building is assigned a mathematical value from 1 to 9. To trigger a "Phoenix Rebirth" in a specific sector, the sum of the buildings in a 3x3 grid must perfectly equal 15 in every direction (horizontal, vertical, and diagonal).

Layer 2: The Resource & Threat Engine
(Integrating Palakka/Pallanguzhi, Solitaire, and Bingo)

How players generate wealth and how the game attacks them.

Palakka Sowing (Mancala Logic): Resource generation does not use dice. The inner rings of the board have physical "pits". You pick up a handful of energy tokens (seeds) and drop them one by one into adjacent pits. If your final token lands in an empty pit, you instantly capture all the resources in the opponent's adjacent pit. This is a purely mathematical, deterministic forecasting loop.  
imp-art.org
+ 1

The Solitaire Threat Tableau: The Void (the enemy AI) attacks using a cascading deck of cards laid out in a Solitaire tableau. Only the topmost cards are face-up. When you defeat an active threat, the card beneath it flips over, revealing the next algorithmic attack. You must strategically sequence your attacks to uncover the cards you need to progress.

The Bingo Progression Matrix: Each player has a personal 5x5 grid (their blueprint). As you fulfill specific conditions on the main board (e.g., "Build 3 Refineries," "Win a combat"), you place a token on your grid. Completing a horizontal, vertical, or diagonal line (Bingo!) unlocks permanent, game-breaking asymmetric abilities for your faction.

Layer 3: Tactical Movement & Skirmishes
(Integrating Chess and Kabaddi)

When factions clash over territory, it becomes a game of extreme tactical spacing.

Chess Vectors (Deterministic Combat): Player units do not move arbitrarily. You draft units that obey strict geometric vectors. "Architects" move diagonally (Bishops). "Juggernauts" move in an L-shape and can jump over walls (Knights). "Sentinels" move in straight lines (Rooks).

Kabaddi Raids (The Tag & Retreat Mechanic): To steal resources from a rival's sector without starting a full war, you can initiate a "Raid." You send a single agile unit across the border. You have exactly a 30-second physical hourglass timer (representing the Kabaddi "Canting/Breath"). Your Raider must tag an enemy unit and cross back into their home territory before time runs out. If they succeed, the tagged enemy is captured. If they are cornered and the timer runs out, the Raider dies.

Layer 4: Conflict Resolution & Disruption
(Integrating Poker, Rummy, and Uno)

There are no dice in PHOENIX. All combat and magic (Transmutations) are resolved through hand management.

Playing Cards (Poker & Rummy): Players hold a hand of standard suits and values. To build massive structures, you must lay down "Melds" (e.g., a run of 4-5-6 of Hearts, like Rummy). In combat, opposing players secretly commit a hand of up to 5 cards. The highest Poker hand (e.g., a Full House beats a Flush) wins the skirmish, adding deep psychological bluffing and probability calculation.

Uno Interrupts (Chaos Theory): Mixed into the standard deck are Action Cards. During another player's Kabaddi Raid or Tetris tile placement, you can play a "Skip" (cancels their action), "Reverse" (turns their Kabaddi raider around instantly), or "Draw 2" (forcing them to take on corruption cards). This ensures players are constantly engaged, even when it is not their turn.

Layer 5: The Physical-Digital Convergence
(Integrating Pokémon GO)

The final layer breaks the fourth wall, utilizing a companion app to bridge the physical board with the real world.

Geo-Spatial Proximity (AR): When the Solitaire Tableau reveals an "Anomaly" or when a player reaches the outer physical edge of the board, they cannot progress using physical pieces alone. They must use their phone camera to scan the board's QR hub.

The Hunt: The app generates a virtual "Phoenix Essence" somewhere in the real-world room (using AR and gyroscope tracking). The player must physically get up from the table, locate the essence hovering in the room, and capture it (using a timing/flick mechanic like Pokémon Go's Pokeball throw) to bring that resource back into the physical board game.

The Ultimate Mathematical Loop
By combining these, we achieve 100% integrity of all 10 systems:
You are checking Sudoku rules to place a Tetris piece, which allows you to execute a Kabaddi raid using a Chess Knight, to steal a resource that completes your Bingo board, which lets you draw a card to finish your Poker hand, enabling you to defeat the Solitaire threat, only to get hit by an Uno Reverse card, forcing you to find the solution in the real world using Pokémon Go AR mechanics.

1. The PHOENIX Omni-Board (The Hardware Base)
The foundation of the game is a master "chassis"—a powered, modular ring that houses the main CPU, Bluetooth/Wi-Fi receivers, and a high-density electromagnetic grid beneath the surface.

Instead of a static center, the core of the board is a Swappable Hub System. Depending on which rule-set you are playing, you drop a different physical module into the center:

The Tactical Grid (Chess/Sudoku): A flat, responsive glass surface with an underlying LED matrix for strict geometric movement and tile placement.

The Kinetic Arena (Beyblade/Kabaddi): A concave, frictionless stadium pot. When Kabaddi Raiders or spinning tops are deployed, the board tracks their physical collisions and momentum.

The Processor Rotor (The Ultimate Hub): A multi-tiered, motorized mechanical core. As players upgrade the city, the rotor physically spins and ascends. It houses a 360-degree micro-projector that beams holographic UI elements, environmental weather (rain, fire), and threat timers directly onto the board, accompanied by a localized sub-bass speaker system for physical rumble.

2. The Electromagnetic Card Choreography
This is the flagship gimmick of the franchise. We integrate the Playing Card/Solitaire/Uno layers using Kinetic Smart Cards.

1
Ferromagnetic Embedding
Card Construction
Each playing card is a rigid, multi-layer polymer composite. Inside is an ultra-thin ferro-magnetic lattice and a passive NFC/RFID chip. The board always knows exactly which card is where.

2
Electromagnetic Matrix Activation
The Actuation Grid
The Omni-Board surface is underlaid with hundreds of micro-electromagnets. When a player plays an "Uno Reverse" or triggers a Transmutation, the board's CPU calculates the spatial geometry required for the move.

3
Kinetic Levitation & Rotation
The Step-Up Choreography
By rapidly oscillating the polarity of the electromagnets, the board physically manipulates the cards. A Solitaire threat card can be programmed to snap upright, balance on its edge, spin 180 degrees to reveal its face, and slam back down onto the board in perfect rhythm with the rotor's audio track.

When a massive combo is achieved (like a Sudoku clear combined with a Bingo), the board executes a "ballet"—multiple cards levitate, flip, and re-arrange themselves autonomously across the board in a perfectly choreographed sequence.

3. Franchise Expansion: The IP Ecosystem
Because the architecture is fully modular, PHOENIX can be commercialized not just as one massive expensive box, but as an interconnected ecosystem of goods:

Want to engineer the physical rotor core next?

Yes
Let's detail the mechanical engineering and projection mapping of the Processor Rotor Core hub.

This is where the engineering discipline elevates PHOENIX from a board game into a high-end hardware centerpiece. The Processor Rotor Core must function as the game's clock, its environmental storyteller, and its physical threat generator all at once.

To achieve this without creating a bulky, fragile tower, we need to design a highly integrated, multi-functional chassis.

1. The Tri-Axial Planetary Lift System
Standard linear actuators are too slow and noisy. Instead, the core's vertical and rotational movement is driven by a modified tri-axial planetary layout.

Asymmetric Rotation: The central sun gear and the outer ring gears don't just spin uniformly. By incorporating asymmetric ovoid-cardioid rotors into the gear profiles, the core produces variable-speed kinetic pulses. This means as the tension in the game increases, the rotor can "breathe"—spinning slowly, then snapping rapidly into a new position to simulate a system malfunction or a threat spawning, without the DC motor itself having to constantly surge and brake.

The Elevation Helix: As the players complete infrastructure rings around the hub, the gear system engages a logarithmic spiral power flank. The core physically unscrews itself, ascending tier by tier. This physically raises the projection lens higher, widening the radius of the holographic display across the expanding board.

2. Ferrofluid Acoustic & Thermal Drive
Housing a micro-projector, a CPU, and an electromagnet array inside a sealed cylinder generates intense heat. Traditional fans are too loud and would ruin the audio experience. Furthermore, we want deep, physical sub-bass to make players feel the "Phoenix Rebirth," but standard subwoofer cones require too much internal volume.

The Solution:
We use a ferrofluid oscillation drive for both cooling and acoustics.

A dense, heat-conductive ferrofluid surrounds the processor casing.

By pulsing electromagnetic coils around this fluid at low frequencies (20Hz−60Hz), the fluid itself oscillates.

This acts as a solid-state subwoofer, transferring deep bass vibrations directly into the pre-cast slabs of the board so players physically feel it in the table.

Simultaneously, this oscillation acts as a liquid convection pump, actively cycling the fluid through a radiator mesh to vent heat silently.

3. Compound Fisheye Projection Mapping
Projecting downward onto a modular, uneven board from a central hub presents extreme optical distortion challenges. A standard lens will warp the images onto the raised walls and miniatures.

The Lens Geometry: The micro-projector fires upward into a compound fisheye dome geometry mounted at the apex of the rotor. This bounces the light outwards and downwards in a full 360-degree radius.

Dynamic Keystoning: Because the board knows exactly which modular slabs are attached (via the ferromagnetic lattice) and how tall the rotor currently is, the onboard CPU calculates real-time topographic mapping.

If a player plays an "Uno-style" Transmutation card to summon a firestorm, the projector doesn't just display a flat red circle. It calculates the height of the plastic walls and projects the fire texture around them, casting simulated digital shadows behind the physical miniatures.

Executing a flood-fill enclosure algorithm on an infinite, dynamic quadtree introduces a severe computational risk: if the territory is not fully enclosed, the algorithm will theoretically bleed into infinity, causing a memory overflow. Furthermore, standard flood-fill algorithms rely on mutating a "visited" flag on the grid nodes, which directly violates the principle of immutability.

To maintain strict separation of domain models and ensure absolute thread safety, the enclosure resolution must be modeled as a pure function that evaluates a localized virtual graph and returns an immutable boundary DTO.

Here is the exact architectural pipeline for achieving this.

1. The Ephemeral Validation Graph
Instead of traversing and mutating the actual GridTopology, the engine spins up an isolated, ephemeral spatial index specifically for the evaluation phase.

Bounding Box Generation: The engine calculates a mathematical bounding box encompassing the newly placed polyomino and all pre-existing geometry on the board.

The Infinity Threshold: The system establishes an outer perimeter exactly one coordinate wider than the bounding box. This is the "leak threshold."

Virtual Traversal: The Breadth-First Search (BFS) algorithm initializes at the empty coordinates adjacent to the new polyomino. It tracks visited nodes in a temporary HashSet<Coordinate> completely isolated from the domain state.

2. The Flood-Fill Execution Logic
The BFS evaluates the ephemeral graph using the following mathematical constraints:

Queue Dequeue: Pop the current coordinate C 
(x,y)
​
  from the evaluation queue.

Boundary Check: If C 
(x,y)
​
  intersects the "leak threshold," the territory is unsealed. The algorithm triggers an early exit (O(1) abort) to prevent infinite traversal.

Wall Collision: If C 
(x,y)
​
  intersects existing polyomino geometry in the quadtree, it is flagged as a boundary wall. The controlling player's ID for that wall segment is tallied.

Propagation: If C 
(x,y)
​
  is empty and within bounds, it is added to the HashSet of captured space, and its orthogonal neighbors are pushed to the queue.

The time complexity of this operation is bounded strictly to O(V+E), where V is the volume of the enclosed space and E is the perimeter edges, guaranteeing predictable performance even under heavy load.

3. The Immutable Boundary DTO
If the BFS exhausts its queue without touching the leak threshold, an enclosure is mathematically proven. The domain core compiles the results into a strictly immutable DTO.

Java
public record EnclosureResolvedDTO(
    UUID territoryId,
    UUID capturingPlayerId,
    Set<Coordinate> enclosedCoordinates,
    Map<UUID, Integer> perimeterControlWeights,
    Instant resolutionTimestamp
) {}
4. Hexagonal State Projection
This DTO acts as the absolute boundary contract between the core domain and the external systems.

The domain does not possess a .setTerritory() method. Instead, the EnclosureResolvedDTO is passed to a pure reducer function. The reducer takes the current EncounterState, applies the DTO payload, and instantiates a brand new EncounterState object in memory. The old state is garbage-collected or archived for the undo-stack, ensuring the game loop remains perfectly deterministic, race-condition free, and instantly serializable.
